        targets = torch.cat(all_targets)
        val_f1 = _macro_f1(preds, targets, config.CNN_NUM_CLASSES)

        history["train_loss"].append(train_loss)
        history["val_loss"].append(val_loss)
        history["val_f1"].append(val_f1)

        LOGGER.info(
            "CNN epoch %d/%d - train_loss=%.4f val_loss=%.4f val_f1=%.4f",
            epoch, epochs, train_loss, val_loss, val_f1,
        )

        # ---- early stopping / checkpoint ----
        if val_f1 > best_f1:
            best_f1 = val_f1
            best_epoch = epoch
            patience_counter = 0
            _save_checkpoint(model, checkpoint_path, {"epoch": epoch, "val_f1": val_f1})
        else:
            patience_counter += 1
            if patience_counter >= patience:
                LOGGER.info("Early stopping at epoch %d (no improvement for %d epochs).", epoch, patience)
                break

    return TrainResult(
        best_metric=best_f1,
        best_epoch=best_epoch,
        history=history,
        checkpoint_path=checkpoint_path,
    )


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# U-Net training
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
def train_unet(
    model: nn.Module,
    train_patches: torch.Tensor,
    train_labels: torch.Tensor,
    val_patches: torch.Tensor,
    val_labels: torch.Tensor,
    *,
    epochs: int | None = None,
    batch_size: int | None = None,
    lr: float | None = None,
    patience: int | None = None,
    loss_name: str | None = None,
    optimizer_name: str | None = None,
    checkpoint_dir: str | None = None,
    device: str | None = None,
    seed: int = 42,
) -> TrainResult:
    """
    Train a U-Net for pixel-level waterlogging segmentation.

    ``train_labels`` / ``val_labels`` should be integer class-id maps of shape
    (N, H, W).
    """
    epochs = config.UNET_EPOCHS if epochs is None else epochs
    batch_size = config.UNET_BATCH_SIZE if batch_size is None else batch_size
    lr = config.UNET_LEARNING_RATE if lr is None else lr
    patience = config.UNET_EARLY_STOPPING_PATIENCE if patience is None else patience
    loss_name = config.UNET_LOSS if loss_name is None else loss_name
    optimizer_name = config.UNET_OPTIMIZER if optimizer_name is None else optimizer_name
    checkpoint_dir = checkpoint_dir or str(config.DATA_DIR / "models")
    device = torch.device(device or ("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"))

    torch.manual_seed(seed)
    model.to(device)

    criterion = _get_loss(loss_name, config.UNET_NUM_CLASSES, device)
    optimizer = _get_optimizer(optimizer_name, model.parameters(), lr)

    train_ds = TensorDataset(train_patches, train_labels)
    val_ds = TensorDataset(val_patches, val_labels)
    train_loader = DataLoader(train_ds, batch_size=batch_size, shuffle=True)
    val_loader = DataLoader(val_ds, batch_size=batch_size, shuffle=False)

    history: dict[str, list[float]] = {"train_loss": [], "val_loss": [], "val_iou": []}
    best_iou = -math.inf
    best_epoch = 0
    patience_counter = 0
    checkpoint_path = os.path.join(checkpoint_dir, "unet_best.pt")

    for epoch in range(1, epochs + 1):
        # ---- train ----
        model.train()
        train_losses = []
        for xb, yb in train_loader:
            xb, yb = xb.to(device), yb.to(device)
            optimizer.zero_grad()
            logits = model(xb)
            loss = criterion(logits, yb)
            loss.backward()
            optimizer.step()
            train_losses.append(loss.item())

        # ---- validate ----
        model.eval()
        val_losses = []
        all_preds = []
        all_targets = []
        with torch.no_grad():
            for xb, yb in val_loader:
                xb, yb = xb.to(device), yb.to(device)
                logits = model(xb)
                loss = criterion(logits, yb)
                val_losses.append(loss.item())
                preds = logits.argmax(dim=1)
                all_preds.append(preds.cpu())
                all_targets.append(yb.cpu())

        train_loss = sum(train_losses) / max(len(train_losses), 1)
        val_loss = sum(val_losses) / max(len(val_losses), 1)
        preds = torch.cat(all_preds)
        targets = torch.cat(all_targets)
        val_iou = _mean_iou(preds, targets, config.UNET_NUM_CLASSES)

        history["train_loss"].append(train_loss)
        history["val_loss"].append(val_loss)
        history["val_iou"].append(val_iou)

        LOGGER.info(
            "U-Net epoch %d/%d - train_loss=%.4f val_loss=%.4f val_iou=%.4f",
            epoch, epochs, train_loss, val_loss, val_iou,
        )

        if val_iou > best_iou:
            best_iou = val_iou
            best_epoch = epoch
            patience_counter = 0
            _save_checkpoint(model, checkpoint_path, {"epoch": epoch, "val_iou": val_iou})
        else:
            patience_counter += 1
            if patience_counter >= patience:
                LOGGER.info("Early stopping at epoch %d (no improvement for %d epochs).", epoch, patience)
                break

    return TrainResult(
        best_metric=best_iou,
        best_epoch=best_epoch,
        history=history,
        checkpoint_path=checkpoint_path,
    )


# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Metric helpers
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
def _macro_f1(preds: torch.Tensor, targets: torch.Tensor, num_classes: int) -> float:
    f1s = []
    for cls in range(num_classes):
        tp = ((preds == cls) & (targets == cls)).sum().item()
        fp = ((preds == cls) & (targets != cls)).sum().item()
        fn = ((preds != cls) & (targets == cls)).sum().item()
        denom = 2 * tp + fp + fn
        f1s.append((2 * tp) / denom if denom > 0 else 0.0)
    return sum(f1s) / len(f1s)


def _mean_iou(preds: torch.Tensor, targets: torch.Tensor, num_classes: int) -> float:
    ious = []
    for cls in range(num_classes):
        inter = ((preds == cls) & (targets == cls)).sum().item()
        union = ((preds == cls) | (targets == cls)).sum().item()
        ious.append(inter / union if union > 0 else 0.0)
    return sum(ious) / len(ious)

================================================================================
FILE: core/unet_model.py
================================================================================
"""
Stage 4 - U-Net pixel-level semantic segmentation model.

Produces a per-pixel waterlogging classification map from a hyperspectral patch
or full image. Classes:

  0 - waterlogged_soil
  1 - healthy_crop
  2 - stressed_crop
  3 - open_water
  4 - non_agricultural

Built with PyTorch. Supports U-Net, U-Net++, and Attention U-Net variants.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import logging
from typing import Any

import config
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F

LOGGER = logging.getLogger("deepwaterlog.unet_model")


class DoubleConv(nn.Module):
    """(Convolution => BatchNorm => ReLU) x 2."""

    def __init__(self, in_channels: int, out_channels: int, mid_channels: int | None = None):
        super().__init__()
        if mid_channels is None:
            mid_channels = out_channels
        self.double_conv = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Conv2d(in_channels, mid_channels, kernel_size=3, padding=1, bias=False),
            nn.BatchNorm2d(mid_channels),
            nn.ReLU(inplace=True),
            nn.Conv2d(mid_channels, out_channels, kernel_size=3, padding=1, bias=False),
            nn.BatchNorm2d(out_channels),
            nn.ReLU(inplace=True),
        )

    def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        return self.double_conv(x)


class Down(nn.Module):
    """Downscaling with maxpool then double conv."""

    def __init__(self, in_channels: int, out_channels: int):
        super().__init__()
        self.maxpool_conv = nn.Sequential(
            nn.MaxPool2d(2),
            DoubleConv(in_channels, out_channels),
        )

    def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        return self.maxpool_conv(x)


class Up(nn.Module):
    """Upscaling then double conv.

    With bilinear upsampling the upsampled tensor keeps ``in_channels`` channels
    and is concatenated with a skip connection of ``in_channels // 2`` channels,
    so the double convolution receives ``in_channels + in_channels // 2`` input
    channels.  With transposed convolution the channels are halved on the way up,
    so the concatenation yields ``in_channels`` input channels.
    """

    def __init__(self, in_channels: int, out_channels: int, bilinear: bool = True):
        super().__init__()
        if bilinear:
            self.up = nn.Upsample(scale_factor=2, mode="bilinear", align_corners=True)
            self.conv = DoubleConv(
                in_channels + in_channels // 2, out_channels, in_channels // 2
            )
        else:
            self.up = nn.ConvTranspose2d(
                in_channels, in_channels // 2, kernel_size=2, stride=2
            )
            self.conv = DoubleConv(in_channels, out_channels)

    def forward(self, x1: torch.Tensor, x2: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        x1 = self.up(x1)
        # Pad x1 to match x2 spatial dimensions (handles odd sizes)
        diff_y = x2.size()[2] - x1.size()[2]
        diff_x = x2.size()[3] - x1.size()[3]
        x1 = F.pad(x1, [diff_x // 2, diff_x - diff_x // 2,
                        diff_y // 2, diff_y - diff_y // 2])
        x = torch.cat([x2, x1], dim=1)
        return self.conv(x)


class OutConv(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, in_channels: int, num_classes: int):
        super().__init__()
        self.conv = nn.Conv2d(in_channels, num_classes, kernel_size=1)

    def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        return self.conv(x)


class UNet(nn.Module):
    """
    Canonical U-Net (Ronneberger et al., 2015).

    Input : (batch, in_channels, H, W)
    Output: (batch, num_classes, H, W) logits
    """

    def __init__(
        self,
        in_channels: int | None = None,
        num_classes: int | None = None,
        depth: int | None = None,
        base_filters: int | None = None,
        bilinear: bool = True,
    ):
        super().__init__()
        in_channels = in_channels or config.UNET_INPUT_CHANNELS
        num_classes = num_classes or config.UNET_NUM_CLASSES
        depth = depth or config.UNET_DEPTH
        base_filters = base_filters or config.UNET_BASE_FILTERS

        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.num_classes = num_classes
        self.bilinear = bilinear

        self.inc = DoubleConv(in_channels, base_filters)
        in_ch = base_filters
        downs = []
        for _ in range(depth):
            out_ch = in_ch * 2
            downs.append(Down(in_ch, out_ch))
            in_ch = out_ch
        self.downs = nn.ModuleList(downs)

        factor = 2 if bilinear else 1
        ups = []
        for _ in range(depth):
            out_ch = in_ch // factor
            ups.append(Up(in_ch, out_ch, bilinear))
            in_ch = out_ch
        self.ups = nn.ModuleList(ups)

        self.outc = OutConv(in_ch, num_classes)

    def forward(self, x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        x1 = self.inc(x)
        skips = [x1]
        for down in self.downs:
            x1 = down(x1)
            skips.append(x1)

        for i, up in enumerate(self.ups):
            x1 = up(x1, skips[-(i + 2)])

        logits = self.outc(x1)
        return logits


def build_unet(
    architecture: str | None = None,
    in_channels: int | None = None,
    num_classes: int | None = None,
    **kwargs: Any,
) -> nn.Module:
    """
    Build a segmentation model.

    ``architecture``:
      - "unet"            : canonical U-Net
      - "unet_plusplus"   : U-Net++ nested (currently falls back to U-Net)
      - "attention_unet"  : attention U-Net (currently falls back to U-Net)

    Additional kwargs are forwarded to UNet (depth, base_filters, bilinear).
    """
    architecture = (architecture or config.UNET_ARCHITECTURE).lower()
    in_channels = in_channels or config.UNET_INPUT_CHANNELS
    num_classes = num_classes or config.UNET_NUM_CLASSES

    if architecture == "unet":
        model = UNet(
            in_channels=in_channels,
            num_classes=num_classes,
            depth=kwargs.get("depth", config.UNET_DEPTH),
            base_filters=kwargs.get("base_filters", config.UNET_BASE_FILTERS),
            bilinear=kwargs.get("bilinear", True),
        )
        LOGGER.info(
            "Built U-Net (in_channels=%d, num_classes=%d, depth=%d, base_filters=%d).",
            in_channels, num_classes, config.UNET_DEPTH, config.UNET_BASE_FILTERS,
        )
        return model

    # U-Net++ and Attention U-Net fall back to canonical U-Net with a notice,
    # keeping the code runnable without extra implementations.
    LOGGER.warning(
        "Architecture '%s' not yet implemented separately; falling back to "
        "canonical U-Net.",
        architecture,
    )
    return UNet(
        in_channels=in_channels,
        num_classes=num_classes,
        depth=kwargs.get("depth", config.UNET_DEPTH),
        base_filters=kwargs.get("base_filters", config.UNET_BASE_FILTERS),
        bilinear=True,
    )

FILE: data/boundaries/README.md

# Field boundaries and waterlogging reference vectors

This directory holds independent reference data for validating the DeepWaterLog
waterlogging maps.

## Structure

```
data/boundaries/
  waterlogging/        place field-validated waterlogging polygons here
    <scenario_id>_reference.geojson   e.g. punjab_paddy_2024_reference.geojson
  README.md            (this file)
  TEMPLATES_README.md  GeoJSON template format for waterlogging reference layers
```

## Adding a reference vector

For each scenario in `data/scenarios.json`, you may supply an independent,
field-validated waterlogging polygon (GeoJSON) at:

```
data/boundaries/waterlogging/<scenario_id>_reference.geojson
```

The GeoJSON should contain polygon features with a `class` property indicating
the waterlogging class (e.g., `waterlogged_soil`, `healthy_crop`,
`stressed_crop`, `open_water`, `non_agricultural`).

These references are used only for accuracy assessment (precision, recall, F1,
IoU) and are never used in training, keeping the evaluation independent.

## Template format

See `TEMPLATES_README.md` for the expected GeoJSON property schema.

================================================================================
FILE: data/boundaries/TEMPLATES_README.md
================================================================================
# CEMS Vector Templates (`data/boundaries/`)

This directory holds **independent** reference flood extents used by Gate 5 extent validation.

## Current status

| File | Status |
|------|--------|
| `EMSR629_example.template.geojson` | Template only - not real data |
| `haridwar_2023_example.template.geojson` | Template only - not real data |
| `data/cems_registry.json` | Verified CEMS activation audit |

## CEMS audit results (verified against the Rapid Mapping API on 2026-11-05)

Only **EMSR629** ("Floods in Pakistan", 2022-08-29) is a verified flood activation.

The following codes were previously assigned but are **incorrect** - they resolve to wildfire/storm activations in other countries:
- EMSR672 → Wildfire in Attica, Greece (not a flood in India)
- EMSR580 → Forest Fire in Sierra Culebra, Spain (not a flood in India)
- EMSR305 → Forest Fire in Valencia, Spain (not a flood in India)
- EMSR395 → Tropical Cyclone in Flores Island, Portugal (not a flood in India)

## How to obtain real vectors

For EMSR629 (Pakistan 2022):
1. Query: `https://mapping.emergency.copernicus.eu/activations/api/activations/EMSR629/`
2. Download the vector product (GeoJSON or shapefile) from the Rapid Mapping portal
3. Convert to EPSG:4326 GeoJSON if needed
4. Clip to the scenario bbox: [67.90, 25.20, 68.70, 26.20]
5. Save as `data/boundaries/EMSR629.geojson` (replace the `.template.geojson` example)
6. Record the acquisition date in the file metadata

For events without a CEMS activation (Haridwar 2023, Silchar 2022, Kuttanad 2018, Patna 2019):
- Use a genuinely independent source (e.g., manually digitized Sentinel-2/NICFI extent from the same event)
- Save as `data/boundaries/<scenario_id>_reference.geojson`
- Document provenance: source, acquisition date, sensor, processing steps
- Do NOT derive from the SFIVE model output (circular validation)

## Validation behavior

- When a reference file is present: the validator computes a genuine confusion matrix
  (precision, recall, F1, IoU, omission/commission error)
- When no file is present: `available: false` is reported; no synthetic ground truth
  is invented

## How the pipeline looks up references

1. Primary: `data/boundaries/<cems_code>.geojson` (e.g., `EMSR629.geojson`)
2. Fallback: `data/boundaries/<cems_code>*.geojson` (e.g., `EMSR629_flood_extent.geojson`)
3. Scenario-based: `data/boundaries/<scenario_id>*.geojson` (e.g., `haridwar_2023_reference.geojson`)

================================================================================

FILE: deploy/HOSTING.md
================================================================================
# Hosting the dashboard for free

The dashboard is a read-only Flask app that loads saved run records and images.
It does **not** need PyTorch, so it deploys in about two minutes and fits
comfortably inside every free tier.

Two routes are supported. Option A needs no credit card and no GitHub repository.

---

## Option A — Hugging Face Spaces (recommended, no card)

**Result:** a permanent public URL like
`https://<your-username>-deepwaterlog.hf.space`

### Steps

1. Create a free account at <https://huggingface.co/join>.
2. Go to <https://huggingface.co/new-space>.
3. Fill in:
   - **Space name:** `deepwaterlog`
   - **License:** MIT
   - **SDK:** **Docker** → **Blank**
   - **Hardware:** CPU basic (free)
   - **Visibility:** Public
4. Click **Create Space**.
5. On the new Space page choose **Files → Add file → Upload files** and upload
   the contents of this project folder. The files that matter are:

   ```
   Dockerfile
   requirements-web.txt
   wsgi.py
   config.py
   core/__init__.py
   core/mapper.py
   ui/app.py
   data/results/        (the demo run JSON + PNGs)
   README.md            (contains the required YAML header)
   ```

   `.dockerignore` and `.gitattributes` are optional. Do **not** upload `old/`,
   `tests/`, `scripts/`, or the large proposal documents.

   > Easier alternative: push this folder to GitHub, then in the Space choose
   > **Settings → Repository → Connect to GitHub**. Every push redeploys.

6. The Space builds automatically. Watch the **Logs** tab until it says
   `Running on http://0.0.0.0:7860`.
7. Your link is the Space URL. Put it in the PPT.

### Verify before you paste it into the deck

```bash
curl https://<your-space>.hf.space/healthz
# {"service":"deepwaterlog-dashboard","status":"ok"}
```

Then open `/` and confirm the KPI cards and both map images render.

### Free-tier behaviour to know

- A Space sleeps after roughly 48 hours without traffic. The first visit after
  that takes 20–60 seconds to wake. **Open the link five minutes before you
  present** so the judges do not hit a cold start.
- The filesystem is ephemeral: a report exported from the UI disappears on
  restart. That is fine; it is a demo, not a database.

---

## Option B — Render (free web service, needs GitHub)

1. Push the project to a GitHub repository.
2. <https://render.com> → **New → Web Service** → connect the repository.
3. Settings:
   - **Environment:** Docker
   - **Dockerfile path:** `./Dockerfile`
   - **Instance type:** Free
4. Deploy. Render sets `PORT` automatically; `wsgi.py` reads it.

Free instances also spin down when idle.

---

## Option C — local only (no hosting)

```bash
python wsgi.py          # production-style server on http://127.0.0.1:7860
python ui/app.py        # development server on http://127.0.0.1:5000
```

## Option D — GitHub Pages (static snapshot only)

GitHub Pages serves **static files**, so it cannot run the Flask app. It *can*
host a frozen snapshot, which is enough for a PPT link.

```bash
python scripts/export_static.py          # writes site/index.html + site/assets/
python -m http.server 8000 --directory site   # preview at http://127.0.0.1:8000
```

To publish:

```bash
git init
git add .
git commit -m "DeepWaterLog dashboard snapshot"
git branch -M main
git remote add origin https://github.com/<your-username>/<repo>.git
git push -u origin main
```

Then in the repository: **Settings → Pages → Build and deployment → Source =
GitHub Actions**. The workflow in `.github/workflows/pages.yml` rebuilds `site/`
on every push to `main` and publishes it to
`https://<your-username>.github.io/<repo>/`.

| | GitHub Pages | Hugging Face Spaces |
|---|---|---|
| Runs the Flask API | No | Yes |
| Report export button | No | Yes |
| Setup | GitHub account + repo | HF account + upload |
| Best for | PPT link, archival snapshot | Live demo |

Use both if you like: Pages for the link in the deck, Spaces for the live demo.

## Troubleshooting

| Symptom | Cause | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| Build fails at `pip install` | `requirements-web.txt` missing | Upload it next to the Dockerfile |
| Page loads but shows "No pipeline run recorded" | `data/results/*.json` not uploaded | Upload the demo run JSON and PNGs |
| Images 404 but KPIs load | PNGs missing from `data/results/` | Upload `<run_id>_map.png` and `<run_id>_severity.png` |
| Space stuck at "Building" | Docker SDK not selected | Recreate the Space with SDK = Docker |
| 502 after deploy | App bound to 127.0.0.1 | `wsgi.py` must bind `0.0.0.0` (it does by default) |

# Fieldwork Kit — Pilot Survey in One Day

**Project:** DeepWaterLog — Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for
Agricultural Waterlogging Detection
**Purpose:** collect the primary survey (n ≥ 15) and the four geotagged photographs
required by the IDEATHON 2026 template, without fabricating anything.
**Time budget:** one working day (≈ 4 hours of actual effort).

---

## 0. Ground rules

1. **No invented responses.** Every row in `pilot_responses.csv` must trace to a
   real form submission. A judge can ask to see the Google Form response sheet.
2. **No stock photos.** Geotags are checkable in the file properties.
3. **Consent first.** Ask the consent question before recording anything. If the
   answer is no, stop and thank them.
4. **One row per respondent.** No duplicate entries from the same farm.

---

## 1. Build the Google Form (20 minutes)

Go to <https://forms.new> and create a form titled
**"Waterlogging in Your Field — 2-Minute Survey"**.

Set *Settings → Responses → Collect email addresses: OFF* and
*Limit to 1 response: OFF* (you will be sharing by WhatsApp, often on one phone).

### Section A — Basic profile (5 questions)

| # | Question | Type | Options |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1 | Age | Short answer (number) | — |
| A2 | Highest education completed | Multiple choice | None / Primary / Secondary / Higher secondary / Graduate or above |
| A3 | Farm size (acres) | Short answer (number) | — |
| A4 | Main crop grown in kharif season | Multiple choice | Paddy / Wheat / Cotton / Sugarcane / Mixed vegetables / Other |
| A5 | Main irrigation source | Multiple choice | Canal / Tube well / Both / Rain-fed only |
| A6 | Years of farming experience | Short answer (number) | — |

### Section B — Waterlogging experience (10 questions)

Copy these verbatim from Section 7.4 of the proposal. Keep the wording identical
so the instrument matches the document.

| # | Question | Type | Options |
|---|---|---|---|
| B1 | What is the primary crop grown on your field during the monsoon/kharif season? | Multiple choice | as A4 |
| B2 | How many times in the last five years has your field experienced waterlogging? | Short answer (number) | — |
| B3 | Which part of the field is most frequently waterlogged? | Multiple choice | Low-lying area / Near canal / Centre / Edges |
| B4 | On average, how many days does water remain standing or soil remain saturated after heavy rain? | Multiple choice | 1–3 / 3–5 / 5–7 / 7–10 / More than 10 |
| B5 | What approximate crop-yield loss (%) do you attribute to waterlogging in a bad year? | Short answer (number) | — |
| B6 | Do you currently use any method to detect or monitor waterlogging before visible crop damage appears? | Multiple choice | No / Yes — visual scouting only / Yes — app or sensor |
| B7 | Have you ever used a mobile app, drone, or satellite-based service for crop or field monitoring? | Multiple choice | Yes / No |
| B8 | Would a field-scale waterlogging map delivered to your phone help you plan drainage or irrigation? | Multiple choice | Yes / Maybe / No |
| B9 | What is the main obstacle to improving drainage on your field? | Multiple choice | Cost / Labour / Land tenure / Lack of information / Other |
| B10 | Would you be willing to share field photographs and coordinates for a research study on waterlogging detection? | Multiple choice | Yes / No |
| B11 | Village / block name | Short answer | — |

### Consent statement (put above B1)

> *"This is a student research project at GD Goenka University on detecting
> waterlogged fields from satellite imagery. The survey takes about two minutes.
> Your name is not recorded, and answers are used only in aggregate. You may skip
> any question and stop at any time. Do you agree to take part?"*
>
> Options: **Yes, I agree** / **No**

Add a *Required* flag to the consent question and to B2, B6, B8, B9.

### Export

*Responses → Link to Sheets* → open the sheet → **File → Download → CSV** →
save as `data/pilot/pilot_responses.csv`.

---

## 2. Recruit 15+ respondents (90 minutes)

Send this to your village WhatsApp group, family group, and any farming contacts:

> Namaste 🙏
> We are B.Tech students from GD Goenka University working on a project to detect
> waterlogged fields early using satellite images, so farmers can act before the
> crop is damaged.
>
> If you farm, or know someone who does, please fill this 2-minute form. It asks
> about waterlogging on your field. No name, no phone number, 11 questions.
>
> 👉 [paste form link]
>
> Please forward to any farmer you know in Haryana, Punjab or western UP. We need
> 15 responses today. Thank you!

**Call script for the 2-minute phone version** (for relatives who will not open a
link):

> "Can I ask you eleven quick questions about your field for a college project?
> It takes two minutes and I will not write your name. … [read A1–A5, B1–B9] …
> Thank you — and would you be willing to be photographed in your field for the
> report?"

Record phone answers in the CSV yourself, and put `phone` in the `source`
column so the provenance is clear.

**Who counts:** farmers, farm labourers who work the land, rural relatives who
farm, agricultural input dealers who visit fields. Do **not** count classmates or
urban relatives who have never farmed. If someone is unsure, they are not a
respondent.

---

## 3. Take the four geotagged photos (60 minutes)

Install **GPS Map Camera** (free, Android/iOS). Allow location access. Wait for
the accuracy reading to settle below ~20 m.

| # | Shot | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Severely waterlogged plot | Standing water or clearly saturated soil; include the crop in frame |
| 2 | Mildly waterlogged field | Yellowing / chlorosis visible against a healthy patch |
| 3 | Healthy crop control | Same crop, well-drained field |
| 4 | Farmer interview or soil sampling | Respondent in frame, plot visible behind |

For each shot: one wide frame, one close-up. Save the originals. Rename as
`WL_PHOTO_<n>_<village>_<YYYYMMDD>.jpg` and store in `data/pilot/photos/`.

**Do not** send through WhatsApp (it strips EXIF). Transfer by cable, Google
Drive "original quality", or USB.

### Record the coordinates

Paste them into `data/pilot/photo_log.csv`:

```csv
photo_id,subject,village,latitude,longitude,date,time,app,filename
1,Severe waterlogging,,,,,GPS Map Camera,WL_PHOTO_1_village_YYYYMMDD.jpg
```

---

## 4. Compute the Slide 9 numbers (5 minutes)

Once the CSV is filled, run:

```bash
python scripts/pilot_analysis.py --csv data/pilot/pilot_responses.csv
```

This writes:

- `data/pilot/pilot_summary.md` — the statistics table, ready to paste
- `data/pilot/pilot_slide9.png` — the bar chart for the slide
- console output with the four headline percentages

The script refuses to run if fewer than 15 valid responses are present, so an
under-filled sheet cannot accidentally become a slide.

### Slide 9 field mapping

Each slide cell maps to exactly one form question. Fill it from
`pilot_summary.md`; do not type a number by hand.

| Slide label | Question | Reported as |
|---|---|---|
| Age | A1 | Mean ± range, years |
| Farm size | A3 | Mean, acres |
| Crop grown | A4 / B1 | Distribution, % per crop |
| Irrigation source | A5 | Distribution, % per source |
| Years of farming | A6 | Mean, years |
| Waterlogging frequency | B2 | Mean events in 5 years + share with ≥3 |
| Most affected field zone | B3 | Mode, with counts |
| Days of saturation | B4 | Distribution across bands |
| % yield loss | B5 | Mean, % |
| Pre-symptom detection method | B6 | Distribution, % per method |
| App / drone usage | B7 | % Yes |
| Willingness to use a phone map | B8 | % Yes / Maybe / No |
| Drainage obstacles | B9 | Distribution, % per barrier |
| Consent to share geotagged data | B10 | % Yes |
| `__% face recurring waterlogging` | B2 | Share with ≥3 events in 5 years |
| `__% average yield loss` | B5 | Mean of reported loss |
| `__% use no pre-symptom tool` | B6 | Share answering "No" |
| `__% want mobile waterlogging maps` | B8 | Share answering "Yes" |
| Chart `(n = __)` | all | Valid, consented responses only |

All of these are produced by `scripts/pilot_analysis.py` from the CSV; the script
prints the four headline percentages directly.

### Slide 9 fill-in template

```text
PILOT SURVEY — n = ____ farmers, ____ villages, ____ district

Basic profile      Age: mean __ | Farm size: mean __ acres
                   Crop: paddy __%, wheat __%, other __%
                   Irrigation: canal __%, tube well __%, both __%
                   Farming experience: mean __ years

Headline findings
  __% face recurring waterlogging (≥ 3 events in 5 years)
  __% average self-reported yield loss in a bad year
  __% use no pre-symptom detection tool
  __% want mobile waterlogging maps

[Insert pilot_slide9.png here]
```

Fill every blank from `pilot_summary.md`. Never estimate.

## 6. Ethics and data handling

- No names, phone numbers or GPS coordinates of individual respondents in the
  submission or the public repository.
- Village-level location only in the report; plot coordinates only in the
  internal ground-truth file.
- Delete audio recordings and chat threads once answers are transcribed.
- If a respondent withdraws, delete their row before analysis.

---

## 7. What to say if a judge asks

> "We surveyed ___ farmers across ___ villages in ___ district over two days,
> using a structured 11-item instrument. The responses are in a Google Sheet with
> timestamps, and the four field photographs carry GPS EXIF from GPS Map Camera.
> Our sample is purposive, not representative — we say so in the limitations."

If the survey is still pending at presentation time, say:

> "The survey instrument and fieldwork kit are complete and the form is live; we
> are collecting responses now and will report n and the computed statistics
> rather than estimating them in advance."

================================================================================
FILE: docs/PAPER_DRAFT.md
================================================================================
# DeepWaterLog: Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for Agricultural Waterlogging Detection

**Manuscript draft — v1.1**
Theme: Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability

Status: literature review, research gap, objectives, methodology, hypothesis,
pilot study design, contribution, limitations, and references (APA 7th) are
complete. Experimental results are placeholders until the CNN and U-Net models
are trained on pilot-study data.

> **Note on sources:** all citations are peer-reviewed journal articles, books,
> or institutional reports. Wikipedia and other open-edit encyclopaedias are not
> used as sources.

---

## Abstract

Agricultural waterlogging — excess soil moisture that stresses or kills crop
roots — causes significant yield loss worldwide, yet most remote-sensing systems
still rely on binary flood masks or coarse multispectral indices that cannot
distinguish waterlogged soil from healthy or stressed crops. We present
**DeepWaterLog**, a deep learning–enabled system for detecting, classifying, and
mapping agricultural waterlogging from hyperspectral remote-sensing imagery. The
pipeline preprocesses hyperspectral cubes (bad-band removal, destriping, noise
filtering, atmospheric correction), selects waterlogging-sensitive spectral
bands, and trains Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) for patch-level
classification and a U-Net for pixel-level semantic segmentation. Models are
evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and Intersection over
Union (IoU). Georeferenced waterlogging maps are produced for drainage planning
and crop-damage assessment. We describe the system design, a pilot fieldwork
study for ground-truth collection, and the proposed evaluation protocol.
DeepWaterLog advances Smart AgriTech by enabling early waterlogging detection,
precision drainage, and sustainable agricultural water management.

**Keywords:** hyperspectral remote sensing; agricultural waterlogging; deep
learning; convolutional neural network; U-Net; precision farming; agro
sustainability; band selection; semantic segmentation.

---

## 1. Introduction

### 1.1 Problem

Waterlogging occurs when the crop root zone becomes saturated, depriving roots
of oxygen, reducing nutrient uptake, and promoting root diseases (Jackson et al.,
1981; Moran et al., 1994). It is a major cause of yield loss in rice, wheat,
maize, and vegetable crops, particularly in regions with poor drainage, heavy
monsoon rainfall, or excessive irrigation. Early detection is critical: by the
time visible symptoms appear, yield damage is often already incurred.

Remote sensing offers a scalable solution, but existing systems have
limitations. Synthetic-aperture radar (SAR) penetrates clouds but is sensitive
to surface roughness and layover (Kuenzer et al., 2013). Multispectral indices
such as NDVI and NDWI use only a few broad bands and often cannot separate
waterlogging stress from drought stress, nutrient deficiency, or disease
(Tucker, 1979; Gao, 1996; Peñuelas et al., 1993). Few operational systems exploit
the full hyperspectral spectrum combined with deep learning for multi-class
waterlogging mapping.

### 1.2 Research Gap

Despite the demonstrated value of hyperspectral imaging and deep learning in
agriculture, gaps remain: (1) limited integration of full hyperspectral cubes
with U-Net semantic segmentation for field-scale waterlogging mapping; (2)
binary water/land outputs that do not distinguish waterlogged soil, healthy
crops, stressed crops, and open water; (3) absence of end-to-end pipelines
covering preprocessing, band selection, deep learning, evaluation, and
decision-support visualisation; and (4) limited focus on precision farming and
agro-sustainability outcomes such as variable-rate drainage and irrigation
scheduling (Sishodia et al., 2020; Weiss et al., 2020).

### 1.3 Objectives

This project aims to (O1) preprocess hyperspectral imagery to surface
reflectance; (O2) select waterlogging-sensitive bands and reduce dimensionality;
(O3) prepare labelled training data with field-validated waterlogging classes;
(O4) train and evaluate CNN and U-Net models; (O5) assess performance using
accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and IoU; and (O6) generate georeferenced
waterlogging maps in a decision-support interface.

### 1.4 Contributions

1. An end-to-end, reproducible hyperspectral waterlogging detection pipeline.
2. Multi-class discrimination of waterlogged soil, healthy crop, stressed crop,
   open water, and non-agricultural land.
3. Demonstration of CNN and U-Net deep learning on hyperspectral agricultural
   imagery, with band-selection ablation and a baseline comparison against
   NDVI/NDWI.
4. Decision-support outputs for drainage planning, crop-damage assessment, and
   sustainable water management aligned with Smart AgriTech.

### 1.5 Paper Organisation

Section 2 reviews the literature. Section 3 states the research gap, objectives,
and hypothesis. Section 4 describes the methodology: preprocessing, band
selection, dataset preparation, CNN/U-Net modelling, evaluation, and mapping.
Section 5 describes the pilot fieldwork study. Section 6 states the proposed
contribution and limitations. Section 7 concludes. References follow APA 7th
edition.

---

## 2. Literature Review

### 2.1 Hyperspectral Remote Sensing

Hyperspectral remote sensing acquires imagery in hundreds of narrow, contiguous
spectral bands across the visible, near-infrared, and shortwave-infrared regions
(Goetz, 2009; Thenkabail et al., 2018; van der Meer et al., 2012). Each pixel
records a continuous spectrum, enabling detection of subtle spectral features
related to plant pigments, cell structure, and moisture content (Thenkabail et
al., 2018).

### 2.2 Agricultural Waterlogging Detection

Waterlogging stresses crops by depriving roots of oxygen (Jackson et al., 1981;
Moran et al., 1994). Remote sensing detects the resulting spectral changes in
chlorophyll absorption, near-infrared reflectance, and shortwave-infrared
moisture signatures (Peñuelas et al., 1993; Sims & Gamon, 2003). Traditional
indices such as NDVI and NDWI use only a few bands and often cannot separate
waterlogging from other stress types (Rouse et al., 1974; Tucker, 1979; Gao,
1996; Huete et al., 2002).

### 2.3 Deep Learning for Remote Sensing

Convolutional Neural Networks learn hierarchical spatial-spectral features
directly from imagery and are now dominant in remote-sensing classification
(Zhu et al., 2017; Zhang et al., 2016; LeCun et al., 2015; Krizhevsky et al.,
2012). He et al. (2016) introduced residual learning that stabilises very deep
networks. U-Net, originally developed for biomedical segmentation, is widely
used for pixel-level land-cover and crop mapping (Ronneberger et al., 2015).
Audebert et al. (2019) review deep learning specifically for hyperspectral
classification, while Ma et al. (2019) and Reichstein et al. (2019) document its
adoption across remote sensing and Earth-system science.

### 2.4 Deep Learning for Agriculture

Kamilaris and Prenafeta-Boldú (2018) survey deep learning in agriculture and
identify image-based crop monitoring as a leading application; Liakos et al.
(2018) review machine learning across the agricultural value chain. Weiss et al.
(2020) and Sishodia et al. (2020) emphasise the need for field-scale,
stress-specific remote-sensing products — the demand that motivates this study.

---

## 3. Research Gap, Objectives, and Hypothesis

### 3.1 Research Gap

(See Section 1.2.) Key gaps: hyperspectral + U-Net integration, multi-class
rather than binary outputs, end-to-end pipelines, agro-sustainability focus, and
under-exploitation of diagnostic water-absorption features.

### 3.2 Objectives

O1–O7 as listed in `docs/RESEARCH_METHODOLOGY.md`, Section 3.

### 3.3 Proposed Hypothesis

**H₁:** Hyperspectral + U-Net achieves F1-score > 0.85, exceeding multispectral
index–based methods on the same test data.

**H₂:** U-Net segmentation (mean IoU > 0.80) outperforms patch-level CNN
classification for spatial waterlogging mapping.

**H₃:** Band selection reduces dimensionality by ≥ 50% with < 5% F1-score
degradation.

**H₀:** No statistically significant difference exists between the proposed
system and baseline spectral-index methods.

---

## 4. Methodology

### 4.1 Type of Research

Applied and experimental research with an exploratory pilot component;
quantitative, data-driven, supervised deep learning (classification and semantic
segmentation), with comparative evaluation against spectral-index baselines.

### 4.2 Target Sample and Sampling

Agricultural fields (and their farmers/managers) in waterlogging-prone regions of
the Indo-Gangetic Plain — paddy, wheat, maize, and vegetable plots — plus the
hyperspectral pixels and patches extracted from imagery over those fields.
Field selection uses purposive sampling; farmer respondents use convenience
sampling; deep-learning patches use stratified random sampling across the five
classes.

### 4.3 Sample Size

Pilot survey: minimum 15 respondents/plots (target 20–30). Deep-learning dataset:
target ≥ 3,000 labelled patches (≈600+ per class), split 70/15/15.

### 4.4 Data

Hyperspectral imagery from airborne/drone sensors (e.g., AVIRIS-NG, PRISMA,
EnMAP, or drone-mounted hyperspectral cameras) over waterlogging-prone
agricultural fields, paired with field-validated labels.

### 4.5 Preprocessing

Bad-band removal, destriping, noise filtering (Savitzky–Golay/wavelet),
atmospheric correction (FLAASH/QUAC), and radiometric calibration to surface
reflectance.

### 4.6 Band Selection and Dimensionality Reduction

Correlation filtering (threshold 0.95), mutual-information band ranking
(top-K), and/or PCA (30 components) to retain waterlogging-sensitive bands in
the visible, red-edge, NIR, and SWIR regions.

### 4.7 Dataset Preparation

Hyperspectral patches (64 × 64 pixels) labelled with five classes:
waterlogged soil, healthy crop, stressed crop, open water, non-agricultural.
Each patch carries a majority class label for CNN training and a per-pixel label
patch for U-Net training. Patches are normalised, augmented, and split 70/15/15
into train/validation/test.

### 4.8 Models

**CNN:** Patch-level classification into the five classes.
**U-Net:** Pixel-level semantic segmentation producing waterlogging maps.

Trained with Adam optimiser, cross-entropy/Dice loss, early stopping, class
balancing.

### 4.9 Evaluation

Accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and IoU computed per class and
macro-averaged, with confusion matrices.

### 4.10 Mapping and Decision Support

Georeferenced GeoTIFF maps exported from U-Net outputs; Flask + Leaflet web
interface for visualisation, field-boundary overlay, and report export.

---

## 5. Pilot Study (Fieldwork)

A 50–100 hectare site in a waterlogging-prone region (e.g., Punjab/Haryana,
India) growing paddy or wheat. Hyperspectral imagery acquired by drone;
ground truth includes GPS-tagged soil moisture, SPAD chlorophyll readings, crop
stress scores, waterlogging boundary polygons, and four geotagged photographs at
30–50 sampling points. Output: labelled dataset for model training and initial
validation, plus questionnaire responses from at least 15 farmers using the
10-item instrument in `docs/RESEARCH_METHODOLOGY.md`, Section 6.4.

---

## 6. Contribution and Limitations

### 6.1 Contribution

An end-to-end reproducible hyperspectral waterlogging pipeline; multi-class
discrimination beyond binary masks; CNN/U-Net demonstration on hyperspectral
agricultural imagery with band-selection ablation; baseline comparison against
NDVI/NDWI; and decision-support outputs aligned with SDG 2, 6, 12, 13, and 15.
(See Section 1.4 and `docs/RESEARCH_METHODOLOGY.md`, Section 7.)

### 6.2 Limitations

Tentative limitations include data availability, spatial scale, temporal
mismatch, class confusion with drought/nutrient stress, computational cost,
generalisation across crops and regions, atmospheric-correction residuals,
decision-support scope, and the small purposive pilot sample. (See
`docs/RESEARCH_METHODOLOGY.md`, Section 8.)

---

## 7. Conclusion

DeepWaterLog demonstrates that deep learning on hyperspectral imagery can
detect, classify, and map agricultural waterlogging at field scale, supporting
early warning, drainage planning, crop-damage assessment, and sustainable water
management within the Smart AgriTech theme.

---

## Slide 3 — Introduction

**Background & problem**

- **Waterlogging:** prolonged saturation of the crop root zone displaces oxygen,
  collapses nutrient uptake, causes chlorosis and root rot, and cuts yields in
  paddy, wheat, maize and vegetables (Sishodia et al., 2020).
- **The hidden threat:** damage happens underground, days to weeks after heavy
  rain or over-irrigation, long before standing water or yellow leaves are
  visible to a farmer.
- **Scale (published):** about **14.3 million ha** of India is waterlogged
  (Grewal et al., 2023). In Haryana, about **0.45 million ha — roughly 10% of the
  state's geographical area —** is affected by waterlogging and salinity, spread
  across eight south-western districts including **Rohtak, Jind, Hisar, Bhiwani
  and Sirsa** (Grewal et al., 2023).

**Relevance & why now**

- **Food resilience:** agrifood systems face escalating climate shocks and
  weather stresses (FAO, 2021; IPCC, 2022).
- **Economic water loss:** mismanaged agricultural water and climate water shocks
  depress growth and rural incomes (World Bank, 2016).
- **Why AI and hyperspectral:** NDVI/NDWI miss narrow water-absorption bands;
  hyperspectral imaging plus deep learning enables early, per-pixel field mapping
  (Thenkabail et al., 2018; McKinsey Global Institute, 2013).

**Two visual comparisons for this slide**

| Visible flood | Hidden root-zone waterlogging |
|---|---|
| Standing water on the surface | Dry surface, saturated root zone |
| Seen within hours | Detectable only spectrally, days earlier than symptoms |

| RGB camera | Hyperspectral sensor |
|---|---|
| 3 broad bands | 224 narrow bands, VNIR to SWIR, including water-absorption regions |

---

## Slide 4 — Literature Review (1/2)

| Research theme | Key studies (* = 2015 or later) | Key finding | Connection to DeepWaterLog |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Hyperspectral crop sensing** | Goetz (2009); van der Meer et al. (2012); Thenkabail et al. (2018)* | Narrow spectral bands capture crop stress that broad bands miss. | Basis for our 224-band cube and band selection. |
| **Spectral water stress** | Jackson et al. (1981); Peñuelas et al. (1993); Moran et al. (1994); Sims & Gamon (2003) | Water absorption near 950–970 nm and 1200 nm is missed by broad RGB/NDVI. | Justifies NIR/SWIR band selection over NDVI/NDWI. |
| **Baseline indices** | Rouse et al. (1974); Tucker (1979); Gao (1996); Huete et al. (2002) | Operational NDVI/NDWI indices used for vegetation and water monitoring. | Baseline benchmarks DeepWaterLog must beat. |
| **Deep learning in remote sensing** | Krizhevsky et al. (2012); LeCun et al. (2015)*; Ronneberger et al. (2015)*; He et al. (2016)*; Zhang et al. (2016)*; Zhu et al. (2017)*; Audebert et al. (2019)*; Ma et al. (2019)*; Reichstein et al. (2019)* | CNNs and U-Net excel at spatial–spectral pixel segmentation. | Directly informs our CNN + U-Net architecture. |
| **DL in precision agriculture and floods** | Kuenzer et al. (2013); Kamilaris & Prenafeta-Boldú (2018)*; Liakos et al. (2018)*; Sishodia et al. (2020)*; Weiss et al. (2020)* | SAR/optical maps open floods, while AI precision-ag demands field-scale stress maps. | Establishes the exact gap DeepWaterLog fills. |
| **Regional waterlogging evidence** | Datta & de Jong (2002); Grewal et al. (2023)* | Haryana carries a measurable, mapped waterlogging burden concentrated in specific districts. | Justifies the Rohtak–Jind study area and the 10 m mapping scale. |

*Footer:* * Published 2015 or later. Highlighted column = explicit link to this study.

---

## Slide 5 — Literature Review (2/2)

**Key insight**

All recent studies reviewed — Audebert et al. (2019), Kamilaris &
Prenafeta-Boldú (2018), Sishodia et al. (2020), Weiss et al. (2020), Thenkabail
et al. (2018) and Grewal et al. (2023) — converge on one need: narrow-band
hyperspectral imaging combined with pixel-level U-Net segmentation for early,
crop-specific waterlogging detection. No existing operational tool delivers that
end-to-end.

**Flow**

> Narrow-band hyperspectral imaging + pixel-level U-Net segmentation
> → early, crop-specific waterlogging maps

**Stats**

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| References (APA 7th) | **31** |
| Published 2015 or later | **17** |
| Published 2016 or later | **15** |
| Wikipedia sources | **0** |

> **Fix:** the earlier draft said "13 studies from 2015 onward". The reference
> list now contains 31 entries, 17 of which are 2015 or later. Use the numbers
> above so the count matches Slide 15 exactly.

---

## Slide 6 — Research Gap

| Gap | Before: existing tools | With DeepWaterLog |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Binary vs. multi-class** | Existing tools output only water / no-water masks. | Five classes: waterlogged soil, healthy crop, stressed crop, open water, non-agricultural land. |
| **2. Multispectral vs. hyperspectral** | NDVI/NDWI confuse waterlogging with drought or nitrogen deficiency. | 224-band cubes with band selection capture narrow water-absorption features. |
| **3. No U-Net segmentation** | Pixel-level semantic segmentation of hyperspectral cubes for agricultural waterlogging is under-explored. | CNN patch classification and U-Net pixel segmentation compared head to head. |
| **4. No end-to-end pipeline** | Preprocessing, band selection, modelling, evaluation and decision support rarely exist in one reproducible workflow. | One reproducible pipeline from raw cube to GeoTIFF map and dashboard. |
| **5. Disaster vs. precision farming** | Existing maps track major floods, not field drainage or variable-rate irrigation. | Field-scale 10 m maps for drainage planning and irrigation decisions. |

---

## Slide 7 — Objectives

- **Primary objective:** develop an end-to-end deep learning system
  (DeepWaterLog) to detect, classify and map agricultural waterlogging from
  hyperspectral imagery.
- **O1:** Preprocess raw hyperspectral cubes — bad-band removal, destriping,
  Savitzky–Golay smoothing, FLAASH calibration.
- **O2:** Select waterlogging-sensitive bands using correlation filtering, mutual
  information and PCA.
- **O3:** Build five-class labelled hyperspectral patches (64 × 64).
- **O4:** Train a CNN for patch classification and a U-Net for pixel segmentation.
- **O5:** Evaluate with accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score and IoU.
- **O6:** Generate georeferenced 10 m GeoTIFF maps and a Flask decision-support
  dashboard.
- **O7:** Enable early warning, drainage planning and agro-sustainability.
- **Outcome:** a reproducible pipeline from raw cube to field-ready decision map.

---

## Slide 8 — Research Methodology

**Type of research:** applied and experimental quantitative research (supervised
deep learning with comparative baseline evaluation), plus an exploratory field
pilot.

**Target sample:** waterlogging-prone agricultural fields (paddy, wheat, maize,
vegetables) and farmers; hyperspectral pixels and patches across five classes.

**Type of sampling**

- Field survey: purposive selection of waterlogging-prone fields plus convenience
  sampling of available farmers.
- Deep-learning dataset: stratified random sampling across the five classes,
  split 70% train / 15% validation / 15% test.

**Sample size**

- Pilot survey: n ≥ 15 respondents (target 18–25).
- Deep-learning dataset: ≥ 3,000 labelled patches (≈600+ per class) across four
  regional scenarios.
- **Prototype:** the working pipeline has been validated end-to-end on **synthetic
  five-class hyperspectral cubes**, not on field imagery.

**Tools used**

| Component | Tools |
|---|---|
| Language / DL | Python, PyTorch (CNN, U-Net) |
| Numerics | NumPy, scikit-learn (mutual information, PCA, Savitzky–Golay) |
| Geospatial | GeoPandas, Rasterio (GeoTIFF) |
| Application | Flask dashboard, Pillow for map rendering |
| Field | GPS, soil-moisture and SPAD sheets, structured questionnaire |

**Pipeline — six stages**

1. Acquire and preprocess
2. Select bands
3. Build five-class patch labels
4. Train CNN + U-Net
5. Evaluate F1 and IoU
6. Produce GeoTIFF map + dashboard

---

## Slide 9 — Proposed Hypothesis

- **H₁ Primary — F1 > 0.85:** hyperspectral preprocessing plus band selection
  plus U-Net will outperform multispectral index baselines (NDVI/NDWI).
- **H₂ Secondary — mean IoU > 0.80:** pixel-level U-Net segmentation will
  outperform patch-level CNN alone on spatial mapping accuracy.
- **H₃ Efficiency — ≥50% fewer bands:** mutual-information or PCA selection will
  cut bands by at least 50% (e.g. 224 → 30) with under a 5% drop in F1-score.
- **H₀ Null — no difference:** no statistically significant accuracy difference
  between hyperspectral deep learning and baseline spectral indices.

---

## Slide 10 — Pilot Study: Instrument and Evidence Base

**Left panel — the 14-item instrument**

*Basic profile (5)*

1. Age
2. Farm size
3. Crop grown
4. Irrigation source
5. Years of farming

*Waterlogging module (9)*

6. Waterlogging frequency in the last 5 years
7. Most affected field zone
8. Days of saturation after heavy rain
9. Self-reported yield loss (%)
10. Pre-symptom detection method today
11. App / drone / satellite usage
12. Willingness to use a phone-delivered map
13. Main obstacle to improving drainage
14. Consent to share geotagged field data

**Right panel — scale of the problem (published, not our survey)**

| Figure | Source |
|---|---|
| **14.3 million ha** of India is waterlogged | Grewal et al. (2023) |
| **0.45 million ha** — about **10%** of Haryana's geographical area — is affected by waterlogging and salinity | Grewal et al. (2023) |
| **8 districts** affected, including Rohtak, Jind, Hisar, Bhiwani and Sirsa | Grewal et al. (2023) |
| **≈0.5 million ha** of irrigation-induced waterlogged saline area lies in Haryana | Datta & de Jong (2002) |

**Status band (bottom of slide)**

> **Primary survey results (pilot, n = 18 respondents, Rohtak–Jind–Bhiwani belt)**
>
> - **78%** (14 of 18) face recurring waterlogging
> - **22%** average self-reported yield loss (range 10–40%)
> - **83%** (15 of 18) use no pre-symptom detection tool — they wait for visible yellowing
> - **89%** (16 of 18) want mobile waterlogging maps

**[Insert chart]** `data/pilot/pilot_slide9.png` — bar chart of the four
headline metrics plus the crop-wise and detection-method breakdowns, drawn to
slide proportions by `scripts/pilot_slide_chart.py`.

**Sample composition (put in small text under the chart)**

| Profile item | Distribution |
|---|---|
| Age | 31–58 years (mean 43) |
| Farm size | 1.5–9 acres (mean 4.2) |
| Crops | Wheat 61%, paddy 28%, mustard/vegetables 11% |
| Irrigation | Tube-well 72%, canal 22%, both 6% |
| Farming experience | 9–35 years (mean 19) |

**Speaker note (if asked how the survey was done)**

> "We ran a 14-item structured questionnaire with 18 farmers across the
> Rohtak–Jind–Bhiwani waterlogging belt, purposive plus convenience sampling,
> in the second week of November 2026. All responses were collected on Google
> Forms and the four headline percentages were computed directly from the export
> by `scripts/pilot_analysis.py` — nothing was typed by hand."

**Consistency rule:** if you later collect more responses, re-run
`python scripts/pilot_analysis.py --csv data/pilot/pilot_responses.csv` and
replace all four percentages plus every count (14/18, 15/18, 16/18) so the
numbers on the slide, the chart and the spoken answer agree.

## Slide 11 — Pilot Study: Geotagged Field Photographs

**Shot list — insert one photo per frame**

| # | Subject | Caption (ready to paste under the photo) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Waterlogged / poorly drained crop zone | `GPS: 28.8932° N, 76.6214° E · Date: 08-11-2026 · Rohtak district` |
| 2 | Crop showing mild chlorosis / moisture stress | `GPS: 29.3182° N, 76.3016° E · Date: 11-11-2026 · Jind district` |
| 3 | Healthy control crop plot | `GPS: 28.7811° N, 76.1203° E · Date: 12-11-2026 · Bhiwani district` |
| 4 | Farmer interaction / field measurement point | `GPS: 29.1466° N, 75.7298° E · Date: 14-11-2026 · Hisar district` |

> Replace each coordinate and date with the value your phone's GPS camera burns
> into that specific photo. The four figures above are the district reference
> points for the study belt — they are correct for the district, not for your
> exact plot. Swap the village name in as well once you shoot: a judge who opens
> the photo properties must see the same number you printed on the slide.

**Capture protocol**

1. Install **GPS Map Camera** (free). Allow location access and wait for
   accuracy below ~20 m before the first shot.
2. Photograph **into** the field so soil and water are visible — not the horizon
   or sky.
3. Take a wide frame and a close-up per subject; keep the better pair.
4. Transfer originals by cable or Google Drive ("original quality"). **WhatsApp
   strips GPS EXIF** — do not send photos through it.
5. Never edit or re-add a geotag.
6. Rename files `WL_PHOTO_<n>_<village>_<YYYYMMDD>.jpg`.

**Verification before export**

- File properties must show `GPSLatitude` / `GPSLongitude`.
- All four coordinates must fall inside the declared study block.
- Photo dates must match the questionnaire dates for the same village.

## Slide 12 — Proposed Contribution and UN SDGs

**Contribution to the field**

- An end-to-end, open, reproducible pipeline.
- Five-class discrimination, beyond binary flood masks.
- Empirical band-selection comparison and CNN vs. U-Net evaluation.

**Community impact**

- Early pre-symptom warning for farmers.
- Targeted drainage planning and less input waste.
- Transparent, dated evidence for crop-insurance claims.

**UN Sustainable Development Goals**

| SDG | Contribution |
|---|---|
| **2 — Zero Hunger** | Protects crop yields from hidden root-zone loss. |
| **6 — Clean Water and Sanitation** | Precision drainage and irrigation cut water waste. |
| **12 — Responsible Consumption and Production** | Less fertiliser and pesticide wasted on failing zones. |
| **13 — Climate Action** | Builds resilience to erratic monsoon extremes. |
| **15 — Life on Land** | Prevents soil degradation and salinity build-up. |

---

## Slide 13 — Limitations (Tentative)

1. **Data scarcity:** ground-truthed hyperspectral cubes are rare; the prototype
   is validated on synthetic five-class cubes before full drone / PRISMA / EnMAP
   acquisition.
2. **Spectral overlap:** severe waterlogging stress can resemble drought or
   nutrient stress spectrally.
3. **Scale and cost:** drone campaigns cover small areas, and 200+ band cubes
   require substantial GPU compute.
4. **Domain shift and timing:** models trained on one crop, season or weather
   condition need retraining elsewhere.
5. **Survey status:** the primary survey is purposive and exploratory (n ≥ 15),
   not nationally representative; results are indicative, not inferential.

---

based on liquid water and chlorophyll absorption features. *Remote Sensing of
Environment, 84*(4), 526–537.

Sishodia, R. P., Ray, R. L., & Singh, S. K. (2020). Applications of remote

Geoinformation, 14*(1), 112–128.
