# DeepWaterLog documentation index

DeepWaterLog — **Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for
Agricultural Waterlogging Detection** — is a reproducible pipeline that
preprocesses hyperspectral cubes, selects waterlogging-sensitive bands, trains
CNN and U-Net models, evaluates them with accuracy, precision, recall, F1 and
IoU, and produces georeferenced waterlogging maps for decision support.

**Theme:** Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability

## Start here

| Document | Purpose |
|---|---|
| [`../IDEATHON_2026_PROPOSAL.md`](../IDEATHON_2026_PROPOSAL.md) | IDEATHON 2026 submission format: introduction, literature review (31 APA references), research gap, objectives, methodology, hypothesis, pilot instrument and collection plan, SDG contribution, limitations, references |
| [`RESEARCH_METHODOLOGY.md`](RESEARCH_METHODOLOGY.md) | Full methodology note: literature review, gap, objectives, six-stage method, hypothesis, pilot fieldwork design, contribution, limitations and APA 7th references. Source for a thesis or manuscript Methods section |
| [`PAPER_DRAFT.md`](PAPER_DRAFT.md) | Manuscript skeleton with abstract, introduction, literature review, methodology, pilot design, contribution, limitations and references. Experimental numbers are placeholders until models are trained on pilot data |
| [`WHAT_AND_WHY.md`](WHAT_AND_WHY.md) | Plain-language explainer: the problem, why hyperspectral, why deep learning, the six stages, honest status, beneficiaries, SDGs, likely judge questions and a glossary |
| [`FIELDWORK_KIT.md`](FIELDWORK_KIT.md) | One-day kit to collect the primary survey honestly: Google Form questions, consent text, WhatsApp recruitment message, photo protocol, Slide 9 template and the analysis command |
| [`PPT_CONTENT_FILLED.md`](PPT_CONTENT_FILLED.md) | All 16 presentation slides written out, with cited numbers, speaker notes and a pre-submission checklist |
| [`../README.md`](../README.md) | Project overview, quick start and repository layout |
| [`../PROJECT_EXPLANATION.txt`](../PROJECT_EXPLANATION.txt) | Short plain-language summary for non-specialist readers |

## Pipeline at a glance

| Stage | Module | Output |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Preprocessing | `core/preprocessing.py` | Surface-reflectance cube (bad bands removed, destriped, denoised, atmospherically corrected) |
| 2. Band selection | `core/band_selection.py` | Reduced band set or PCA components retaining waterlogging-sensitive features |
| 3. Dataset preparation | `core/dataset.py` | Normalised patch tensors with majority labels (CNN) and per-pixel label patches (U-Net), split 70/15/15 |
| 4. Deep learning | `core/cnn_model.py`, `core/unet_model.py`, `core/trainer.py` | Trained CNN classifier and U-Net segmenter with early stopping and checkpoints |
| 5. Evaluation | `core/evaluator.py` | Accuracy, precision, recall, F1, IoU per class and macro-averaged, plus confusion matrices |
| 6. Mapping and decision support | `core/mapper.py`, `ui/app.py` | Georeferenced waterlogging extent/severity maps, Markdown reports, Flask + Leaflet dashboard |
| Orchestration | `core/pipeline.py` | One JSON-serialisable result with provenance and timings |
| Reporting | `core/reporting.py` | `data/results/<run_id>.json` plus a compact line in `run_log.jsonl` |

## Quick start

```bash
# Runtime dependencies (Python 3.12)
uv sync

# Offline test suite (no torch required for most tests)
uv run --with pytest pytest

# End-to-end health check on synthetic hyperspectral data
uv run python test_pipeline.py --bands 30 --size 64 --patch-size 16 --stride 8

# Fast demo run (~35 s) that fills the dashboard with metrics and maps
uv run python scripts/run_demo.py --epochs 10 --size 96 --bands 40

# Train CNN and U-Net models on a labelled .npz dataset
uv run python scripts/train.py --data data/training/scene.npz --model both

# Evaluate trained models
uv run python scripts/evaluate.py --data data/testing/scene.npz --model both

# Launch the decision-support UI
uv run python ui/app.py
```

The dashboard reads the newest record in `data/results/` and displays the
metrics, the classified extent map, the waterlogging severity map, the per-class
area breakdown and a Markdown report export.

### Deploy the dashboard for free

The dashboard needs only Flask, NumPy and Pillow — not PyTorch — so it deploys in
about two minutes. See [`../deploy/HOSTING.md`](../deploy/HOSTING.md) for
Hugging Face Spaces (no card required), Render, and GitHub Pages. Verify a
deployment with:

```bash
curl https://<your-space>.hf.space/healthz
```

GitHub Pages cannot run Flask, so it hosts a frozen snapshot instead:

```bash
python scripts/export_static.py
# site/index.html + site/assets/ are published by .github/workflows/pages.yml
```

### Collect the pilot survey

[`FIELDWORK_KIT.md`](FIELDWORK_KIT.md) contains the Google Form questions, consent
text, recruitment message, photo protocol and the analysis command. Once real
responses exist:

```bash
python scripts/pilot_analysis.py --csv data/pilot/pilot_responses.csv
```

It writes `pilot_summary.md` and `pilot_slide9.png`, and refuses to run below 15
valid responses.

## Data layout

```
data/
  scenarios.json      four waterlogging scenarios (crop, region, window)
  hyperspectral/      raw or surface-reflectance hyperspectral cubes
  labels/             field-validated class maps (0-4)
  boundaries/         independent reference polygons for accuracy assessment
  models/             trained checkpoints (cnn_best.pt, unet_best.pt)
  results/            run records, run_log.jsonl, maps and reports
```

## Class scheme

| Index | Class | Colour |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | waterlogged_soil | red |
| 1 | healthy_crop | green |
| 2 | stressed_crop | orange |
| 3 | open_water | blue |
| 4 | non_agricultural | grey |

## Scientific contract

1. **No leakage.** Reference polygons in `data/boundaries/` are used only for
   accuracy assessment, never for training.
2. **Frozen parameters.** A configuration snapshot (`config.config_snapshot()`)
   is written into every run record.
3. **Recorded decisions.** Bad-band indices, band-selection method, split sizes,
   early-stopping epochs and all warnings are persisted with the result.
4. **Reproducibility.** Seeded splits, run IDs, UTC timestamps, device and
   timings are stored alongside the metrics.
5. **Honest placeholders.** Any unverified number in the manuscript is written as
   a placeholder, never as a finding.

## Current status and caveats

- The full six-stage pipeline runs end-to-end on synthetic hyperspectral data and
  is covered by an offline test suite.
- `scipy` and `rasterio` are optional: without `scipy` the spectral smoothing step
  passes the cube through unchanged and logs a warning; without `rasterio` maps
  are saved as `.npy` instead of GeoTIFF.
- Live data ingestion requires hyperspectral cubes in `data/hyperspectral/` and
  matching label maps in `data/labels/`.
- The decision-support UI is a screening tool, not a replacement for agronomic
  field inspection or engineering drainage design.

================================================================================
FILE: docs/RESEARCH_METHODOLOGY.md
================================================================================
# DeepWaterLog — Research Methodology

**Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for Agricultural Waterlogging Detection**
Version 1.1 · Methodology note for manuscript preparation

**Theme:** Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability

This document assembles the full research methodology for the DeepWaterLog project:
literature review, research gap, objectives, methodology, proposed hypothesis,
pilot study (fieldwork), proposed contribution, limitations, and references
(APA 7th edition). It is written so that the methodology chapter of a thesis or
paper can be assembled directly from it.

> **Note on sources:** all citations are peer-reviewed journal articles, books,
> or institutional reports. Wikipedia and other open-edit encyclopaedias are not
> used as sources anywhere in this project.

---

## 0. Executive Summary

Agricultural waterlogging — excess soil moisture that stresses or kills crop
roots — causes significant yield loss worldwide. Hyperspectral remote sensing
captures hundreds of narrow spectral bands, providing detailed spectral
signatures that can distinguish waterlogged soil, healthy vegetation, stressed
crops, and open water. DeepWaterLog applies deep learning (CNNs for patch-level
classification and U-Net for pixel-level segmentation) to preprocessed
hyperspectral imagery to detect, classify, and map waterlogged areas. Performance
is evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and Intersection over
Union (IoU). Outputs support early waterlogging detection, crop-damage
assessment, drainage planning, and sustainable agricultural management.

---

## 1. Literature Review

### 1.1 Hyperspectral Remote Sensing

Hyperspectral remote sensing acquires images in hundreds of narrow, contiguous
spectral bands across the visible, near-infrared, and shortwave-infrared regions
(Goetz, 2009; Thenkabail et al., 2018; van der Meer et al., 2012). Unlike
multispectral sensors that record a few broad bands, hyperspectral sensors record
a continuous spectrum for each pixel, enabling identification of subtle spectral
features related to plant pigments, cell structure, water content, and soil
moisture (Thenkabail et al., 2018).

Hyperspectral imaging has been applied to agriculture for crop-type
discrimination, nutrient-status assessment, disease detection, and yield
prediction (Thenkabail et al., 2018). The fine spectral resolution allows
detection of plant stress before it becomes visible, making hyperspectral data
well suited to early waterlogging detection.

### 1.2 Waterlogging and Crop Stress Detection

Waterlogging occurs when the soil root zone becomes saturated, depriving roots
of oxygen and reducing nutrient uptake (Jackson et al., 1981; Moran et al.,
1994). Symptoms include leaf yellowing, stunting, root rot, and reduced yield.
Remote sensing detects waterlogging through changes in canopy spectral
reflectance: stressed vegetation shows reduced chlorophyll absorption in the red
band, altered near-infrared reflectance related to cell structure, and altered
shortwave-infrared signatures related to leaf water content (Peñuelas et al.,
1993; Sims & Gamon, 2003).

Traditional indices such as NDVI (Rouse et al., 1974; Tucker, 1979) and NDWI
(Gao, 1996) use only a few bands and often cannot separate waterlogging stress
from drought stress, nutrient deficiency, or disease (Peñuelas et al., 1993;
Sims & Gamon, 2003). Hyperspectral data, with its continuous spectral sampling,
offers greater discriminatory power for these subtle differences.

### 1.3 Deep Learning for Remote Sensing

Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) have become the dominant approach for
image classification in remote sensing (Zhu et al., 2017; Zhang et al., 2016).
CNNs learn hierarchical spatial-spectral features directly from image data,
avoiding the need for hand-crafted indices (Krizhevsky et al., 2012; LeCun et
al., 2015; He et al., 2016).

U-Net, originally developed for biomedical image segmentation (Ronneberger et
al., 2015), has been widely adapted to remote sensing for land-cover mapping,
crop segmentation, and disaster damage assessment. Its encoder–decoder structure
with skip connections preserves spatial detail while learning contextual
features, making it suitable for pixel-level waterlogging mapping. Audebert et
al. (2019) review deep learning specifically for hyperspectral classification,
and Ma et al. (2019) and Reichstein et al. (2019) document the broader adoption
of deep learning across remote sensing and Earth-system science.

### 1.4 Deep Learning for Agriculture and Precision Farming

Kamilaris and Prenafeta-Boldú (2018) survey deep learning in agriculture and
identify image-based crop monitoring as a leading application. Liakos et al.
(2018) review machine learning across the agricultural value chain, while Weiss
et al. (2020) and Sishodia et al. (2020) provide meta-reviews of remote sensing
for agriculture and precision agriculture respectively, emphasising the need for
field-scale, stress-specific products.

### 1.5 Flood and Waterlogging Mapping

SAR-based flood mapping (Kuenzer et al., 2013) and optical water indices (Gao,
1996) detect open water but not the sub-surface, root-zone waterlogging that
most damages crops. This distinction — between "visible flood" and "hidden
waterlogging stress" — is the central motivation of DeepWaterLog, and no
reviewed study combines full hyperspectral cubes with CNN/U-Net for multi-class
agricultural waterlogging mapping at field scale.

### 1.6 Summary of the Literature

| Theme | Key findings | Sources |
|---|---|---|
| Hyperspectral remote sensing | Hundreds of narrow bands enable subtle spectral-feature detection | Goetz (2009); Thenkabail et al. (2018); van der Meer et al. (2012) |
| Waterlogging detection | Water-absorption features near 950–970 nm and 1200 nm indicate canopy water status; NDVI/NDWI are limited | Peñuelas et al. (1993); Sims & Gamon (2003); Jackson et al. (1981); Tucker (1979); Gao (1996) |
| Deep learning (CNN/U-Net) | CNNs and U-Net achieve state-of-the-art results in image classification and segmentation | Krizhevsky et al. (2012); LeCun et al. (2015); He et al. (2016); Ronneberger et al. (2015) |
| DL in remote sensing | Deep learning is now standard across remote-sensing disciplines | Zhu et al. (2017); Zhang et al. (2016); Ma et al. (2019); Reichstein et al. (2019); Audebert et al. (2019) |
| DL in agriculture | Image-based crop monitoring is a leading DL application | Kamilaris & Prenafeta-Boldú (2018); Liakos et al. (2018); Weiss et al. (2020); Sishodia et al. (2020) |
| Flood mapping | SAR/optical methods map open water, not root-zone waterlogging | Kuenzer et al. (2013); Gao (1996) |
| **Gap** | **Few systems combine hyperspectral preprocessing with CNN/U-Net for field-scale multi-class waterlogging mapping** | — |

---

## 2. Research Gap

Despite the demonstrated value of hyperspectral imaging and deep learning in
agriculture, several gaps remain:

1. **Limited hyperspectral + deep learning integration.** Most waterlogging and
   flood remote-sensing studies use multispectral data (e.g., Sentinel-2,
   Landsat) or SAR backscatter (Kuenzer et al., 2013). Few studies combine full
   hyperspectral cubes with deep semantic-segmentation models (U-Net) for
   field-scale waterlogging mapping.

2. **Binary rather than multi-class mapping.** Existing systems often produce a
   simple flood/non-flood or water/land mask. They do not distinguish
   waterlogged soil from healthy crops, stressed crops, and open water —
   distinctions that matter for drainage planning and crop-damage assessment.

3. **Lack of end-to-end pipelines.** Few systems cover the complete workflow
   from raw hyperspectral preprocessing (noise removal, atmospheric correction,
   band selection) through deep learning modelling to georeferenced map output
   and decision-support visualisation.

4. **Limited agro-sustainability focus.** Existing waterlogging tools are
   typically disaster-response oriented rather than designed for precision
   farming, variable-rate drainage, irrigation scheduling, and sustainable
   agricultural management (Sishodia et al., 2020; Weiss et al., 2020).

5. **Under-exploited hyperspectral specificity.** The diagnostic water-absorption
   features that distinguish waterlogging from drought or nutrient stress
   (Peñuelas et al., 1993; Sims & Gamon, 2003) are rarely exploited by
   operational agricultural monitoring products.

DeepWaterLog addresses these gaps by providing a complete, reproducible,
deep-learning–based hyperspectral system for multi-class waterlogging detection
and mapping, targeted at Smart AgriTech and agro sustainability.

---

## 3. Objectives

The project has the following objectives:

**O1.** To preprocess raw hyperspectral imagery (bad-band removal, destriping,
noise filtering, atmospheric correction, radiometric calibration) to produce
surface-reflectance cubes suitable for deep learning.

**O2.** To select waterlogging-sensitive spectral bands and reduce dimensionality
(using correlation analysis, mutual information, or PCA) while retaining
diagnostic spectral features in the visible, red-edge, NIR, and SWIR regions.

**O3.** To prepare labelled training data pairing hyperspectral patches with
field-validated waterlogging classes (waterlogged soil, healthy crop, stressed
crop, open water, non-agricultural).

**O4.** To train and evaluate a CNN for patch-level waterlogging classification
and a U-Net for pixel-level waterlogging segmentation.

**O5.** To assess model performance using accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score,
and Intersection over Union (IoU).

**O6.** To generate georeferenced waterlogging extent and severity maps and
visualise them in a decision-support web interface for drainage planning and
crop-damage assessment.

**O7.** To align the system with the Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro
Sustainability theme by supporting early detection, sustainable water
management, and reduced crop loss.

---

## 4. Research Methodology

### 4.1 Type of Research

- **Nature:** Applied and experimental research with an exploratory pilot
  component.
- **Approach:** Quantitative and data-driven; supervised deep learning
  (classification and semantic segmentation).
- **Design:** Cross-sectional field study combined with computational modelling,
  including a comparative evaluation against baseline spectral-index methods.

### 4.2 Study Areas and Data

Hyperspectral imagery will be acquired from airborne or spaceborne hyperspectral
sensors (e.g., AVIRIS-NG, PRISMA, EnMAP, or drone-mounted hyperspectral cameras)
over agricultural fields with known waterlogging history. Study areas will
include paddy, wheat, maize, and vegetable fields in the Indo-Gangetic plain and
other waterlogging-prone regions.

Field data will include GPS-tagged soil moisture measurements, crop health
scores, and waterlogging boundary delineations collected during the pilot study
(Section 6).

### 4.3 Target Sample

- **Primary target:** Agricultural fields — and the farmers or farm managers who
  cultivate them — in waterlogging-prone regions of the Indo-Gangetic Plain,
  specifically paddy, wheat, maize, and vegetable plots.
- **Secondary target:** Hyperspectral image pixels and patches extracted from
  drone- or satellite-acquired imagery over those fields.

### 4.4 Type of Sampling

- **Field survey:** Mixed sampling — purposive sampling to select
  waterlogging-prone fields (based on historical drainage and crop-loss records),
  combined with convenience sampling of farmer respondents within the selected
  villages.
- **Deep-learning dataset:** Stratified random sampling of hyperspectral patches
  across the five waterlogging classes, to ensure balanced representation in
  training and evaluation.

### 4.5 Sample Size

- **Pilot field survey:** minimum 15 respondents/plots (target 20–30 for
  robustness).
- **Deep-learning dataset:** target ≥ 3,000 labelled patches across five classes
  (approximately 600+ per class), split 70% training / 15% validation / 15% test.
- **Image sources:** drone-mounted hyperspectral sensor and/or spaceborne
  hyperspectral data (e.g., PRISMA, EnMAP) over four pilot scenarios.

### 4.6 Stage 1 — Hyperspectral Preprocessing

Raw hyperspectral cubes undergo:
- **Bad-band removal:** bands affected by water vapour absorption (around
  1400 nm and 1900 nm) and low signal-to-noise ratio are excluded (Goetz, 2009).
- **Destriping:** along-track striping artefacts are reduced using wavelet- or
  polynomial-based methods.
- **Noise filtering:** Savitzky–Golay or wavelet filtering smooths spectral
  noise while preserving absorption features.
- **Atmospheric correction:** FLAASH or QUAC converts at-sensor radiance to
  surface reflectance.
- **Radiometric calibration:** digital numbers are converted to physical
  reflectance units.

Output: surface-reflectance hyperspectral cube.

### 4.7 Stage 2 — Band Selection and Dimensionality Reduction

To reduce redundancy and computational load:
- **Correlation analysis:** bands with pairwise correlation above 0.95 are
  removed.
- **Mutual information:** the top-K bands most informative about waterlogging
  class labels are retained.
- **PCA:** principal components capturing the majority of variance are computed
  as alternative inputs.

Selected bands/components emphasise chlorophyll (visible), vegetation stress
(red-edge), canopy structure and moisture (NIR), and soil/crop moisture (SWIR),
consistent with the spectral water-stress literature (Peñuelas et al., 1993;
Sims & Gamon, 2003).

### 4.8 Stage 3 — Training Data Preparation

Preprocessed hyperspectral patches (e.g., 64 × 64 pixels) are extracted with a
configurable stride. Each patch is labelled with its majority class for CNN
training and retains a per-pixel label patch for U-Net training, based on field
validation data. Patches are normalised (min–max or z-score), augmented (flips,
rotations, spectral jitter), and split into training (70%), validation (15%),
and test (15%) sets.

### 4.9 Stage 4 — Deep Learning Modelling

**CNN (patch-level classification):** A custom CNN (or ResNet/EfficientNet
backbone) classifies each patch into one of the five classes. The network learns
spectral-spatial features from the selected bands (Krizhevsky et al., 2012;
He et al., 2016).

**U-Net (pixel-level segmentation):** A U-Net with skip connections performs
semantic segmentation, assigning every pixel to a waterlogging class
(Ronneberger et al., 2015). The output is a georeferenced classification map.

Models are trained with the Adam optimiser, cross-entropy (or Dice/focal) loss,
early stopping, and class balancing.

### 4.10 Stage 5 — Evaluation

Models are evaluated on the held-out test set using:
- **Accuracy:** fraction of correctly classified patches/pixels.
- **Precision:** TP / (TP + FP) per class.
- **Recall:** TP / (TP + FN) per class.
- **F1-score:** harmonic mean of precision and recall.
- **Intersection over Union (IoU):** TP / (TP + FP + FN) for segmentation maps.

Per-class and macro-averaged metrics are reported, along with confusion
matrices.

### 4.11 Stage 6 — Mapping and Decision Support

U-Net segmentation outputs are georeferenced to the input imagery coordinates
and exported as GeoTIFF maps. A Flask + Leaflet web interface allows users to:
- visualise waterlogging extent and severity maps,
- overlay field boundaries,
- export maps and reports for drainage planning and crop-damage assessment.

### 4.12 Tools and Techniques

| Component | Tools / Techniques |
|---|---|
| Data acquisition | Drone-mounted hyperspectral camera; PRISMA/EnMAP imagery; GPS for geotagging |
| Field measurements | Soil-moisture meter (volumetric water content), SPAD chlorophyll meter, visual stress scoring sheet, handheld GPS |
| Preprocessing | Python, NumPy, SciPy; bad-band removal, Savitzky–Golay/wavelet filtering, FLAASH/QUAC atmospheric correction |
| Band selection | Correlation analysis, mutual-information ranking, PCA |
| Deep learning | PyTorch; CNN (custom/ResNet/EfficientNet) for classification; U-Net for segmentation |
| Evaluation | Accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, IoU, confusion matrices |
| Mapping and UI | rasterio/GeoPandas for GeoTIFF; Flask + Leaflet decision-support interface |
| Survey instrument | Structured questionnaire (Section 6.4) plus semi-structured farmer interviews |

---

## 5. Proposed Hypothesis

**H₁ (Primary):** A deep learning system combining hyperspectral preprocessing,
band selection, and U-Net segmentation will achieve significantly higher
waterlogging detection accuracy (F1-score > 0.85) than multispectral index–based
methods (e.g., NDVI, NDWI) on the same test data.

**H₂ (Secondary):** A U-Net trained on hyperspectral bands will produce more
accurate pixel-level waterlogging maps (mean IoU > 0.80) than a CNN trained for
patch-level classification alone.

**H₃ (Tertiary):** Band selection (mutual information / PCA) will reduce input
dimensionality by at least 50% with less than 5% degradation in F1-score,
improving computational efficiency without meaningful loss of accuracy.

**H₀ (Null):** There is no statistically significant difference between the
proposed hyperspectral deep-learning system and baseline spectral-index methods.

---

## 6. Pilot Study (Fieldwork)

### 6.1 Purpose

A pilot study will collect ground-truth data to train and validate the deep
learning models, and to confirm that spectral signatures in hyperspectral
imagery correlate with field-observed waterlogging conditions.

### 6.2 Study Site

A 50–100 hectare agricultural block in a waterlogging-prone region (e.g., Punjab
or Haryana, India) growing paddy or wheat will be selected. The site will
include zones with varying drainage quality: well-drained, moderately
waterlogged, severely waterlogged, and open water.

### 6.3 Data Collection

- **Hyperspectral imagery:** Drone- or aircraft-mounted hyperspectral sensor
  flown over the site during the monsoon or post-irrigation period.
- **Field measurements:** GPS-tagged soil moisture (volumetric water content)
  at 30–50 points across moisture zones.
- **Crop health:** Visual stress scores (healthy, mild stress, severe stress)
  and chlorophyll meter (SPAD) readings at the same points.
- **Waterlogging boundaries:** Field-mapped polygons of waterlogged areas using
  a handheld GPS.
- **Photographs:** Four geotagged photographs of crop condition at sampling
  points (severe waterlogging, mild waterlogging with chlorosis, healthy control,
  and farmer interview/soil sampling).

### 6.4 Questionnaire (minimum 15 respondents)

*Ten questions uniquely relevant to the research, preceded by basic demographic
and farm-profile items (age, education, farm size, irrigation source, years of
farming experience).*

1. What is the primary crop grown on your field during the monsoon/kharif season?
2. How many times in the last five years has your field experienced waterlogging?
3. Which part of the field is most frequently waterlogged (low-lying area, near
   canal, centre, edges)?
4. On average, how many days does water remain standing, or the soil remain
   saturated, after heavy rain?
5. What is the approximate crop-yield loss (%) you attribute to waterlogging in
   a bad year?
6. Do you currently use any method to detect or monitor waterlogging before
   visible crop damage appears? (Yes/No; if yes, describe)
7. Have you ever used a mobile app, drone, or satellite-based service for
   crop/field monitoring? (Yes/No)
8. Would a field-scale waterlogging map delivered to your phone help you plan
   drainage or irrigation? (Yes/No/Maybe)
9. What is the main obstacle to improving drainage on your field (cost, labour,
   land tenure, lack of information, other)?
10. Would you be willing to share field photographs or coordinates for a research
    study on waterlogging detection? (Yes/No)

### 6.5 Laboratory Analysis

Soil samples will be analysed for bulk density, texture, and hydraulic
conductivity to contextualise waterlogging severity. Crop tissue samples may be
analysed for nutrient status to rule out non-waterlogging stress causes.

### 6.6 Expected Outcome

A labelled dataset of hyperspectral patches with field-validated waterlogging
classes, sufficient to train and initially validate the CNN and U-Net models.
The pilot study will also refine the class definitions and spectral band
selection criteria.

---

## 7. Proposed Contribution

DeepWaterLog makes the following contributions:

1. **End-to-end hyperspectral waterlogging pipeline.** A complete, reproducible
   workflow from raw hyperspectral imagery to georeferenced waterlogging maps,
   including preprocessing, band selection, deep learning, evaluation, and
   decision-support visualisation.

2. **Multi-class waterlogging discrimination.** Distinguishes waterlogged soil,
   healthy crops, stressed crops, open water, and non-agricultural land — beyond
   binary water/land masks.

3. **Deep learning on hyperspectral data.** Demonstrates CNN and U-Net
   architectures applied to hyperspectral imagery for agricultural waterlogging
   detection, with ablation of band-selection methods and a baseline comparison
   against NDVI/NDWI.

4. **Agro-sustainability decision support.** Maps designed for drainage
   planning, variable-rate irrigation, crop-damage assessment, and insurance
   claims, advancing precision farming and sustainable water use.

5. **Open, reproducible codebase.** All preprocessing, model, training,
   evaluation, and UI code is version-controlled and documented, with a
   configuration snapshot recorded for every run.

### 7.1 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) Targeted

| SDG | Goal | How DeepWaterLog contributes |
|---|---|---|
| **SDG 2** | Zero Hunger | Early waterlogging detection protects crop yield and food supply |
| **SDG 6** | Clean Water and Sanitation | Precision drainage and irrigation reduce water waste and runoff |
| **SDG 12** | Responsible Consumption and Production | Variable-rate inputs reduce fertiliser and pesticide waste |
| **SDG 13** | Climate Action | Builds climate resilience against extreme-rainfall waterlogging |
| **SDG 15** | Life on Land | Protects soil health and supports sustainable land use |

Reference: United Nations, 17 Sustainable Development Goals (https://sdgs.un.org/goals).

---

## 8. Limitations (Tentative)

The following limitations are identified at the proposal stage and may be
revised as the project progresses:

1. **Data availability.** Hyperspectral imagery with matching field labels is
   not always publicly available; the project may rely on drone-acquired data
   from a single study site.

2. **Spatial scale.** Drone or airborne hyperspectral data may not cover large
   regions; scaling to satellite hyperspectral data (e.g., EnMAP, PRISMA)
   requires coarser spatial resolution and may reduce per-field accuracy.

3. **Temporal mismatch.** Hyperspectral acquisitions and field measurements
   may not coincide exactly, introducing label noise.

4. **Class confusion.** Stressed crops from waterlogging may be spectrally
   similar to drought-stressed or nutrient-deficient crops, potentially
   reducing class separability.

5. **Computational cost.** Hyperspectral cubes are large; training CNN/U-Net
   models requires substantial GPU resources and time.

6. **Generalisation.** Models trained on one crop type, region, or season may
   not generalise to others without retraining or transfer learning.

7. **Atmospheric correction.** Residual atmospheric effects may remain after
   correction, affecting spectral consistency across acquisitions.

8. **Decision-support scope.** The web interface is a screening tool, not a
   replacement for agronomic field inspection or engineering drainage design.

9. **Survey limitations.** The pilot sample size (≥15) is small and purposive;
   findings are indicative rather than statistically representative.

---

## 9. Project Timeline (Indicative)

| Phase | Activity | Duration |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Literature review and proposal finalisation | Month 1 |
| 2 | Data acquisition (pilot study) and hyperspectral preprocessing | Months 2–3 |
| 3 | Band selection, dataset preparation, labelling | Months 3–4 |
| 4 | CNN and U-Net model development and training | Months 4–6 |
| 5 | Evaluation (accuracy, precision, recall, F1, IoU) | Month 6 |
| 6 | Map generation and decision-support UI | Month 7 |
| 7 | Report writing, paper draft, and final submission | Months 7–8 |

================================================================================
# What We Are Doing, and Why

**Project:** DeepWaterLog — Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for Agricultural Waterlogging Detection
**Theme:** Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability
**Competition:** IDEATHON 2026, GD Goenka University / Avishkar Centre of Innovation and Entrepreneurship

This document explains the project in plain language: what problem we are
solving, why the problem is worth solving, what we are actually building, how it
works, what exists today, and what still has to be proven. It is written so that
a mentor, a judge, a teammate, or a family member can understand the project in
about ten minutes.

---

## 1. The one-paragraph version

Farmers lose a large share of their crop every year because their field gets
waterlogged — the soil stays saturated, crop roots cannot breathe, and the plant
dies slowly. The damage is usually only noticed once the leaves have already
turned yellow, which is too late. We are building a system that reads
**hyperspectral satellite or drone images** — images that record hundreds of
colours instead of just three — and uses **deep learning** to spot waterlogged
soil, healthy crops, stressed crops, and open water *before* a farmer can see the
damage. The output is a **map of the field** showing exactly which parts are
waterlogged and how badly, so that drainage can be fixed, irrigation can be
adjusted, and crop-insurance claims can be supported.

---

## 2. The problem, in plain terms

### What waterlogging actually is

When it rains heavily, or when a field is irrigated too much, or when the land is
flat and the drainage is poor, water fills the tiny spaces between soil
particles. Normally those spaces hold air, and roots use that air to breathe.
Once they fill with water, the roots suffocate. The plant stops taking up
nutrients, stops growing, and if the condition lasts long enough, it dies.

This is why waterlogging is often called a **hidden flood**. There may be no
visible standing water at all. The field simply looks wet. But underground, the
crop is already in trouble.

### Why it is expensive

| Effect | Consequence for the farmer |
|---|---|
| Root oxygen starvation | Stunted growth, yellowing, reduced grain filling |
| Nutrient lockout | Fertiliser is wasted — the plant cannot absorb it |
| Root rot and disease | Secondary fungal and bacterial infections |
| Delayed sowing or harvest | Missed crop calendar, lower yield |
| Repeated in the same spots | Low-lying areas waterlog every single season |

The last point matters most. Waterlogging is not random. It happens in the same
low-lying patches, near the same blocked drains, season after season. That
predictability is exactly what makes it a solvable remote-sensing problem.

### Why it is hard to catch in time

1. **It is invisible from the road.** No standing water, no dramatic damage — just
   a slightly duller, slightly yellower patch of crop.
2. **Farmers cannot walk every field every week.** A two-hectare plot might take
   an hour to inspect properly. A village has hundreds of plots.
3. **Visible symptoms arrive late.** By the time leaves yellow, yield has already
   been lost. You cannot un-lose it.
4. **Existing satellite indices are too blunt.** The standard tools — NDVI,
   NDWI — use only two or three broad colour bands. They see "this pixel is less
   green than last week," but they cannot tell you *why*. Drought, nutrient
   deficiency, disease, and waterlogging all look similar to them.

That fourth point is the technical heart of our project.

---

## 3. Why hyperspectral imaging

### The difference in one picture

A normal camera records three numbers per pixel: red, green, blue.
A multispectral satellite records maybe ten: a few visible bands, a couple of
near-infrared bands, a couple of shortwave-infrared bands.
A **hyperspectral** sensor records **hundreds** of narrow, continuous bands —
essentially a full spectrum for every single pixel of the image.

Think of it this way:

- A normal photo is like describing a colour as "greenish."
- A hyperspectral image is like having the actual paint formula — every
  ingredient and its exact proportion.

### Why that matters for waterlogging

Water leaves a very specific fingerprint in the spectrum:

| What we look for | Where in the spectrum | Why it signals waterlogging |
|---|---|---|
| Water absorption at ~950–970 nm | Near-infrared | Leaf water content rises in saturated crops |
| Water absorption at ~1200 nm | Near-infrared | Second water-content indicator |
| Reduced red-edge slope | ~700–750 nm | Chlorophyll breakdown from root stress |
| Lower chlorophyll absorption | ~680 nm | Yellowing, reduced photosynthesis |
| Higher shortwave-infrared reflectance from wet soil | ~1550–1750 nm | Surface and root-zone moisture |

A three-band camera cannot see any of this. A hyperspectral sensor sees all of
it, simultaneously, at every pixel. That is the whole reason we use it.

---

## 4. Why deep learning instead of a formula

You *could* try to hand-write a rule: "if band 45 is above 0.3 and band 87 is
below 0.2, call it waterlogged." This does not work well, for three reasons:

1. **The rules differ by crop, region, season, and sensor.** A rule tuned on
   Punjab paddy in July fails on Haryana wheat in February.
2. **The signature is not one number.** It is a *shape* across 200 bands, plus
   the spatial pattern of the neighbouring pixels (waterlogging clusters in
   low-lying areas).
3. **Real data is messy.** Clouds, shadows, sensor noise, mixed pixels at field
   edges. Hand-written rules break immediately.

Deep learning solves this by **learning the rules from labelled examples**. We
show the model thousands of image patches where experts have already marked
"this is waterlogged soil," "this is healthy crop," "this is stressed crop,"
"this is open water," and "this is a road." The model finds the patterns itself.

### The two models we use, and why both

| Model | Job | Output | Why we need it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CNN** (Convolutional Neural Network) | Look at a 64×64 patch of the field and say "this patch is mainly waterlogged" | One label per patch | Fast triage — screen thousands of fields quickly |
| **U-Net** | Look at every pixel and assign it to a class | A full map — every pixel labelled | Precise extent — the farmer needs to know *which corner* of the field, not just that the field has a problem |

The CNN answers *"does this field have a problem?"* The U-Net answers *"where
exactly, and how much?"* The map is what actually gets acted on.

---

## 5. What the system produces

For a given field or scene, the system outputs:

1. **A classified map** — each pixel coloured as waterlogged soil, healthy crop,
   stressed crop, open water, or non-agricultural land.
2. **A severity map** — where the model is most confident waterlogging is
   present.
3. **Area statistics** — how many hectares are waterlogged, and what fraction of
   the total mapped area that represents.
4. **Accuracy metrics** — accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and
   Intersection over Union (IoU), so the numbers can be trusted or challenged.
5. **A downloadable report** — suitable for a drainage plan discussion or an
   insurance claim.

All of this is visible in the project's web dashboard.

---

## 6. How it works — the six stages

We built the system as a pipeline. Each stage feeds the next. Every stage
records what it did, so any result can be reproduced.

### Stage 1 — Preprocessing

Raw hyperspectral data is not directly usable. We clean it:

- **Remove bad bands** — some bands sit in water-vapour absorption regions
  (around 1400 nm and 1900 nm) where the signal is mostly atmospheric noise.
- **Destripe** — sensors produce thin vertical stripes; we suppress them.
- **Denoise** — smooth spectral noise while keeping the absorption features
  that actually matter.
- **Atmospheric correction** — convert "what the sensor saw" into "what the
  ground actually reflects."
- **Radiometric calibration** — convert raw digital numbers into physical
  reflectance values.

Output: a clean **surface-reflectance cube**.

### Stage 2 — Band selection and dimensionality reduction

We start with 200+ bands, but many are redundant or useless for waterlogging.
We thin them out using three complementary methods:

- **Correlation filtering** — if two bands are almost identical, drop one.
- **Mutual information** — keep the bands that are most informative about the
  class labels.
- **PCA (Principal Component Analysis)** — compress the whole cube into a small
  number of components that retain most of the variation.

This makes training faster and reduces overfitting, while keeping the
waterlogging-sensitive spectral features.

### Stage 3 — Training data preparation

We cut the cube into square patches (e.g., 64×64 pixels), assign each patch a
class label from field-validated data, normalise the values, apply augmentation
(flips, rotations, slight spectral jitter), and split everything 70% training /
15% validation / 15% test.

Each patch carries two labels: one **majority class** for the CNN, and one
**per-pixel label map** for the U-Net.

### Stage 4 — Deep learning

- The **CNN** learns to classify whole patches.
- The **U-Net** learns to segment pixels, using an encoder–decoder with skip
  connections so that fine spatial detail survives the compression.

Both are trained with early stopping and class balancing, and the best
checkpoint is saved.

### Stage 5 — Evaluation

We report, per class and averaged:

- Accuracy — how often the model is right overall.
- Precision — when it says "waterlogged," how often is it correct?
- Recall — of all the actually-waterlogged pixels, how many did it find?
- F1-score — the balance between precision and recall.
- IoU — overlap between predicted and true waterlogged regions.

Recall matters most for early warning: missing a waterlogged patch is worse than
raising a false alarm.

### Stage 6 — Mapping and decision support

The trained U-Net is run across the **entire scene** using a sliding window. We
average the predictions across overlapping windows to avoid seam artefacts, then
convert the result into a coloured map, compute areas in hectares, and display
everything in the web dashboard with a report export.

---

## 7. What is real today, and what is still planned

Being honest about status is part of doing research properly.

### Working now

- The complete six-stage pipeline runs end to end.
- Preprocessing, band selection, dataset preparation, CNN, U-Net, trainer,
  evaluator, mapper and reporting are all implemented and documented.
- The dashboard displays metrics, per-class breakdown, the classified map, the
  severity map, and exports a report.
- 16 automated tests pass, covering preprocessing, band selection, dataset
  splitting, metrics, map generation, and model forward passes.
- A full demo run on a synthetic scene completes in about 30 seconds and
  produces a coherent map (U-Net accuracy ≈ 0.92, F1 ≈ 0.79, IoU ≈ 0.72 on that
  synthetic data).

### Not yet done — and openly acknowledged

- **No real field data yet.** The demo runs on a synthetic scene built to mimic
  paddy, wheat and vegetable layouts. Real hyperspectral cubes with field labels
  are the next requirement.
- **No field validation yet.** The pilot instrument (10 unique research questions
  plus 5 demographic items) and a complete one-day fieldwork kit are ready; the
  survey itself has not been administered, so no pilot statistics are reported
  yet. Section 7.5 of the proposal says this explicitly.
- **Generalisation unproven.** A model trained on Punjab paddy has not yet been
  tested on Haryana wheat.
- **Georeferencing is optional.** Without the `rasterio` library the maps are
  saved as plain arrays rather than GeoTIFFs.

None of these are hidden. They are listed as limitations in both the proposal
and the methodology document.

---

## 8. Why this matters — who benefits

### The farmer

Gets an early warning with a map of which part of the field is at risk, weeks
before visible damage. Can open a drain, skip an irrigation cycle, or file an
insurance claim with evidence.

### The drainage planner

Gets a spatial picture of which zones waterlog repeatedly, so that limited
budget goes to the drains that will actually fix the problem, instead of being
spread evenly.

### The crop insurer

Gets an objective, dated, georeferenced record of waterlogging extent — instead
of relying on a field visit weeks after the event.

### The agricultural extension service

Gets a scalable screening tool. One analyst with satellite imagery can flag
hundreds of fields, and field officers only visit the ones that need attention.

### The research community

Gets a reproducible, open pipeline that others can extend to new crops, regions,
and sensors.

---

## 9. Fit with the theme and the SDGs

**Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability**

- **Precision farming:** field-scale, crop-specific information instead of
  village-level guesswork. Variable-rate drainage and irrigation become
  possible.
- **Agro sustainability:** preventing yield loss avoids wasting the water,
  fertiliser, seed, and labour already invested in a crop. Protecting soil from
  prolonged saturation protects long-term soil health.

| SDG | How the project contributes |
|---|---|
| **SDG 2 — Zero Hunger** | Reduces crop loss from a major abiotic stressor |
| **SDG 6 — Clean Water and Sanitation** | Precision drainage and irrigation cut water waste and runoff |
| **SDG 12 — Responsible Consumption and Production** | Avoids wasted inputs on crops that would otherwise be lost |
| **SDG 13 — Climate Action** | Builds resilience to extreme-rainfall waterlogging |
| **SDG 15 — Life on Land** | Protects soil health and sustainable land use |

---

## 10. Questions a judge is likely to ask — and our answers

**"Isn't this just NDVI with extra steps?"**
No. NDVI uses two bands and cannot separate waterlogging from drought or
nutrient stress. We use hundreds of narrow bands specifically because the
water-absorption features at 950–970 nm and 1200 nm are what distinguish
waterlogging from other stressors. That is the entire technical justification
for choosing hyperspectral data.

**"Why not just use Sentinel-2? It's free and covers everything."**
Sentinel-2 has about 13 bands. It is excellent for crop type and general
vigour, but its spectral sampling is too coarse to isolate water-absorption
features reliably. We position hyperspectral as the accuracy benchmark, with
multispectral as the scalable fallback once the method is validated.

**"You don't have field data. Isn't this just a demo?"**
Correct, and we say so plainly. What we have built and demonstrated is the
complete working pipeline plus a rigorous, questionnaire-based fieldwork
protocol. The demo proves the software works end to end. The pilot study is the
next step, and its design is fully specified.

**"How do you know the model is right?"**
Five independent metrics — accuracy, precision, recall, F1, and IoU — plus
confusion matrices, evaluated on a held-out test set that was never used for
training. We also plan a baseline comparison against NDVI/NDWI so the advantage
is measured, not assumed.

**"What happens when it's cloudy?"**
Hyperspectral optical sensors cannot see through cloud. This is a stated
limitation. Part of the reason we report limitations up front is that judges
trust a project that knows its own boundaries.

**"Could a farmer actually use this?"**
The dashboard is browser-based, so it needs no installation, and it will be
hosted at a public URL for the demo. The intended delivery is a per-field map and
a short report, not a raw data product. Cost is the barrier our pilot instrument
is designed to measure, and it is the reason the output is built for a free
satellite-based service rather than a paid drone survey.

---

## 11. Glossary

| Term | Plain meaning |
|---|---|
| **Hyperspectral** | An image with hundreds of narrow colour bands instead of three |
| **Band** | One narrow wavelength slice of the spectrum |
| **Surface reflectance** | The fraction of sunlight a surface bounces back, after removing atmospheric effects |
| **CNN** | A neural network that learns visual patterns using small sliding filters |
| **U-Net** | A neural network shaped like a "U" that compresses an image and rebuilds it, labelling every pixel |
| **Semantic segmentation** | Assigning a class to every pixel in an image |
| **Patch** | A small square crop of the image used as one training example |
| **F1-score** | A single number balancing precision and recall |
| **IoU** | Overlap between predicted and true regions; 1.0 means perfect |
| **Waterlogging** | Soil saturation that suffocates crop roots, with or without visible standing water |
| **NDVI / NDWI** | Common two-band vegetation and water indices used as baselines |

---

| `deploy/HOSTING.md` | How to put the dashboard online for free |
| `README.md` | How to install and run the code |
| `PROJECT_EXPLANATION.txt` | Short plain-language summary |

**Theme:** Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability
**GD Goenka University · Avishkar Centre of Innovation and Entrepreneurship**

================================================================================
FILE: scripts/evaluate.py
================================================================================
"""
Evaluate trained CNN and/or U-Net models on a test hyperspectral dataset.

Usage
-----
    python scripts/evaluate.py --data data/testing --model both
    python scripts/evaluate.py --data data/testing --model unet --checkpoint data/models/unet_best.pt
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import sys
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import torch

BASE_DIR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
if str(BASE_DIR) not in sys.path:
    sys.path.append(str(BASE_DIR))

import config
from core.cnn_model import build_cnn
from core.dataset import HyperspectralWaterloggingDataset
from core.evaluator import (
    evaluate_classification,
    evaluate_segmentation,
    format_metrics_report,
)
from core.unet_model import build_unet


def _load_npz(path: Path):
    with np.load(path) as z:
        return z["cube"].astype(np.float32), z["labels"].astype(np.int64)


def main() -> int:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        description="Evaluate CNN and/or U-Net waterlogging models."
    )
    parser.add_argument("--data", required=True, help="Path to labelled .npz file.")
    parser.add_argument(
        "--model", choices=["cnn", "unet", "both"], default="both",
        help="Which model to evaluate.",
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "--cnn-checkpoint", default=None,
        help="Path to CNN checkpoint (default: data/models/cnn_best.pt).",
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "--unet-checkpoint", default=None,
        help="Path to U-Net checkpoint (default: data/models/unet_best.pt).",
    )
    parser.add_argument("--device", default=None, help="Torch device (cuda/cpu).")
    parser.add_argument("--seed", type=int, default=42, help="Random seed.")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    data_path = Path(args.data)
    if not data_path.exists():
        print(f"Data file not found: {data_path}", file=sys.stderr)
        return 1

    device = args.device or ("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
    print(f"Using device: {device}")
    print(f"Loading data from: {data_path}")

    cube, label_map = _load_npz(data_path)

    ds = HyperspectralWaterloggingDataset(
        cube, label_map,
        patch_size=None, stride=None,
    )
    patches, patch_labels = ds.extract_patches()
    ds.normalise()
    splits = ds.split(seed=args.seed)

    def to_t(x):
        return torch.from_numpy(np.asarray(x, dtype=np.float32))

    test_patches = to_t(splits["test"][0])
    test_labels = torch.from_numpy(splits["test"][1].astype(np.int64))

    if args.model in ("cnn", "both"):
        ckpt = args.cnn_checkpoint or str(
            Path(config.DATA_DIR) / "models" / "cnn_best.pt"
        )
        print(f"\n=== Evaluating CNN ({ckpt}) ===")
        cnn = build_cnn()
        state = torch.load(ckpt, map_location=device)
        cnn.load_state_dict(state["model_state_dict"])
        cnn.to(device)
        cnn.eval()
        with torch.no_grad():
            logits = cnn(test_patches.to(device))
            preds = logits.argmax(dim=1).cpu().numpy()
        metrics = evaluate_classification(
            preds, test_labels.numpy(),
            num_classes=config.CNN_NUM_CLASSES,
            class_names=config.CLASSES,
        )
        print(format_metrics_report(metrics, "CNN", config.CLASSES))

    if args.model in ("unet", "both"):
        ckpt = args.unet_checkpoint or str(
            Path(config.DATA_DIR) / "models" / "unet_best.pt"
        )
        print(f"\n=== Evaluating U-Net ({ckpt}) ===")
        unet = build_unet()
        state = torch.load(ckpt, map_location=device)
        unet.load_state_dict(state["model_state_dict"])
        unet.to(device)
        unet.eval()
        with torch.no_grad():
            logits = unet(test_patches.to(device))
            preds = logits.argmax(dim=1).cpu().numpy()
        metrics = evaluate_segmentation(
            preds, test_labels.numpy(),
            num_classes=config.UNET_NUM_CLASSES,
            class_names=config.CLASSES,
        )
        print(format_metrics_report(metrics, "U-Net", config.CLASSES))

    return 0


if __name__ == "__main__":
    raise SystemExit(main())

================================================================================
FILE: scripts/export_static.py
================================================================================
"""
Export a static snapshot of the DeepWaterLog dashboard.

GitHub Pages serves static files only, so it cannot run the Flask dashboard.
This script bakes the latest run record into a single `index.html` plus the map
images, producing a folder that can be published as-is.

Usage
-----
    python scripts/export_static.py
    python scripts/export_static.py --run-id demo_lowland_paddy_20261117T090836Z
    python scripts/export_static.py --out site

Outputs
-------
    site/index.html
    site/assets/<run_id>_map.png
    site/assets/<run_id>_severity.png
    site/.nojekyll
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import html
import json
import shutil
import sys
from datetime import datetime, timezone
from pathlib import Path

BASE_DIR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
if str(BASE_DIR) not in sys.path:
    sys.path.append(str(BASE_DIR))

import config  # noqa: E402

CLASS_META = [
    (0, "Waterlogged soil", "#d62828"),
    (1, "Healthy crop", "#40916c"),
    (2, "Stressed crop", "#f4a261"),
    (3, "Open water", "#2a6fb7"),
    (4, "Non-agricultural", "#969696"),
]


def _latest_run(results_dir: Path) -> Path | None:
    if not results_dir.exists():
        return None
    files = [p for p in results_dir.glob("*.json") if p.name != "run_log.jsonl"]
    if not files:
        return None
    return max(files, key=lambda p: p.stat().st_mtime)


def _fmt(value, digits: int = 2) -> str:
    if value is None:
        return "&mdash;"
    try:
        return f"{float(value):.{digits}f}"
    except (TypeError, ValueError):
        return html.escape(str(value))


def _pct(value, digits: int = 1) -> str:
    if value is None:
        return "&mdash;"
    return f"{float(value) * 100:.{digits}f}%"


def _kpi(label: str, value: str, unit: str = "") -> str:
    unit_html = f'<span class="kpi-unit">{unit}</span>' if unit else ""
    return (
        f'<div class="card"><div class="kpi-label">{html.escape(label)}</div>'
        f'<div class="kpi-value">{value}{unit_html}</div></div>'
    )


def _legend() -> str:
    items = "".join(
        f'<div class="legend-item"><span class="swatch" style="background:{hex_}" '
        f'></span><span>{html.escape(name)}</span></div>'
        for _, name, hex_ in CLASS_META
    )
    return f'<div class="legend">{items}</div>'


def _class_rows(map_info: dict) -> str:
    counts = map_info.get("class_counts") or {}
    areas = map_info.get("class_areas_ha") or {}
    total = sum(int(v) for v in counts.values()) or 1

    rows = []
    for class_id, name, hex_ in CLASS_META:
        count = int(counts.get(str(class_id), counts.get(class_id, 0)) or 0)
        area = float(areas.get(str(class_id), areas.get(class_id, 0)) or 0)
        share = 100.0 * count / total
        rows.append(
            "<tr>"
            f'<td><span class="swatch inline" style="background:{hex_}"></span>'
            f"{html.escape(name)}</td>"
            f'<td class="num">{count:,}</td>'
            f'<td class="num">{area:.2f}</td>'
            f'<td class="bar-cell"><div class="bar-track"><div class="bar-fill" '
            f'style="width:{share:.1f}%;background:{hex_}"></div></div></td>'
            f'<td class="num">{share:.1f}%</td>'
            "</tr>"
        )
    return "\n".join(rows)


def build_html(record: dict, assets: dict[str, str], generated: str) -> str:
    metrics = record.get("metrics") or {}
    cnn = metrics.get("cnn") or {}
    unet = metrics.get("unet") or {}
    map_info = record.get("map") or {}
    dataset = record.get("dataset") or {}
    band_sel = record.get("band_selection") or {}
    provenance = record.get("provenance") or {}
    warnings = record.get("warnings") or []

    kpis = "".join([
        _kpi("U-Net accuracy", _fmt(unet.get("accuracy"), 4)),
        _kpi("U-Net F1 (macro)", _fmt(unet.get("f1_macro"), 4)),
        _kpi("U-Net IoU (macro)", _fmt(unet.get("iou_macro"), 4)),
        _kpi("CNN F1 (macro)", _fmt(cnn.get("f1_macro"), 4)),
        _kpi("Waterlogged area", _fmt(map_info.get("waterlogged_area_ha")), "ha"),
        _kpi("Waterlogged share", _pct(map_info.get("waterlogged_fraction"))),
    ])

    map_img = (
        f'<img class="map-img" src="assets/{assets["map"]}" '
        'alt="Classified waterlogging map">'
        if assets.get("map")
        else '<p class="empty">No class map stored for this run.</p>'
    )
    sev_img = (
        f'<img class="map-img" src="assets/{assets["severity"]}" '
        'alt="Waterlogging severity map">'
        if assets.get("severity")
        else '<p class="empty">No severity map stored for this run.</p>'
    )

    warn_block = ""
    if warnings:
        items = "".join(f"<li>{html.escape(str(w))}</li>" for w in warnings)
        warn_block = f'<h2>Warnings</h2><div class="warn"><ul>{items}</ul></div>'

    return f"""<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
<meta charset="UTF-8">
<meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
<title>DeepWaterLog - Agricultural Waterlogging Detection</title>
<style>
  :root {{
    --ink: #10263b; --muted: #5b7288; --line: #dfe7ee;
    --bg: #f4f7fa; --card: #ffffff;
  }}
  * {{ margin: 0; padding: 0; box-sizing: border-box; }}
  body {{
    font-family: -apple-system, BlinkMacSystemFont, "Segoe UI", Roboto, sans-serif;
    background: var(--bg); color: var(--ink); line-height: 1.5;
    padding: 24px; max-width: 1280px; margin: 0 auto;
  }}
  header {{ margin-bottom: 18px; }}
  h1 {{ font-size: 24px; letter-spacing: -0.3px; }}
  .sub {{ color: var(--muted); font-size: 13px; margin-top: 4px; }}
  .theme-pill {{
    display: inline-block; margin-top: 8px; padding: 3px 10px;
    background: #e6f2ec; color: #1f6b4a; border-radius: 20px;
    font-size: 11px; font-weight: 700; letter-spacing: 0.4px;
    text-transform: uppercase;
  }}
  .notice {{
    margin-top: 14px; background: #fff8e6; border: 1px solid #f1d9a0;
    color: #7a5a12; border-radius: 8px; padding: 10px 12px; font-size: 12px;
  }}
  .grid {{ display: grid; gap: 14px; }}
  .kpis {{ grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(150px, 1fr)); }}
  .card {{
    background: var(--card); border: 1px solid var(--line);
    border-radius: 10px; padding: 16px;
  }}
  .kpi-label {{
    font-size: 10px; font-weight: 700; letter-spacing: 0.7px;
    text-transform: uppercase; color: var(--muted);
  }}
  .kpi-value {{
    font-size: 26px; font-weight: 700; margin-top: 6px;
    font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
  }}
  .kpi-unit {{ font-size: 12px; font-weight: 600; color: var(--muted); }}
  h2 {{
    font-size: 13px; text-transform: uppercase; letter-spacing: 0.7px;
    color: var(--muted); margin: 26px 0 10px;
  }}
  .two-col {{ grid-template-columns: 1fr 1fr; }}
  @media (max-width: 860px) {{ .two-col {{ grid-template-columns: 1fr; }} }}
  .map-img {{
    width: 100%; display: block; border-radius: 6px;
    image-rendering: pixelated; background: #eef2f6;
  }}
  .legend {{ display: flex; flex-wrap: wrap; gap: 10px; margin-top: 10px; }}
  .legend-item {{ display: flex; align-items: center; gap: 6px; font-size: 12px; }}
  .swatch {{ width: 12px; height: 12px; border-radius: 3px; flex: 0 0 auto; }}
  .swatch.inline {{ display: inline-block; margin-right: 7px; }}
  table {{ width: 100%; border-collapse: collapse; font-size: 13px; }}
  th, td {{ text-align: left; padding: 7px 8px; border-bottom: 1px solid var(--line); }}
  th {{
    font-size: 10px; text-transform: uppercase; letter-spacing: 0.6px;
    color: var(--muted);
  }}
  td.num {{ text-align: right; font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums; }}
  .bar-cell {{ width: 30%; }}
  .bar-track {{ background: #eef2f6; border-radius: 4px; height: 8px; overflow: hidden; }}
  .bar-fill {{ height: 100%; border-radius: 4px; }}
  .empty {{ color: var(--muted); font-size: 14px; }}
  code {{
    background: #eef2f6; padding: 2px 6px; border-radius: 4px;
    font-size: 12px; font-family: ui-monospace, Menlo, Consolas, monospace;
  }}
  .warn ul {{ margin-left: 18px; }}
  .pill {{
    display: inline-block; padding: 1px 8px; border-radius: 10px;
    font-size: 10px; font-weight: 700; letter-spacing: 0.4px;
  }}
  .pill-ok {{ background: #e6f2ec; color: #1f6b4a; }}
  .footer {{
    margin-top: 30px; color: var(--muted); font-size: 11px;
    text-align: center; line-height: 1.7;
  }}
</style>
</head>
<body>

  <h1>DeepWaterLog</h1>
  <div class="sub">
    Deep Learning-Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for

    <strong>Static snapshot.</strong> This page is a frozen export of the dashboard
    for GitHub Pages. The interactive version runs as a Flask service.
    Demonstration metrics come from a <strong>simulated five-class hyperspectral
    scene</strong>, not a drone or satellite survey.
  </div>

{kpis}
