# 5. RESEARCH METHODOLOGY

## 5.1 Type of Research

- **Nature:** Applied and experimental research (with an exploratory pilot component).
- **Approach:** Quantitative, data-driven; supervised deep learning (classification + semantic segmentation).
- **Design:** Cross-sectional field study combined with computational modelling; comparative evaluation against baseline spectral-index methods.

## 5.2 Target Sample

- **Primary target:** Agricultural fields (and the farmers/managers who cultivate them) in waterlogging-prone regions of the Indo-Gangetic Plain — specifically paddy, wheat, maize, and vegetable plots.
- **Secondary target:** Hyperspectral image pixels/patches extracted from drone- or satellite-acquired imagery over the target fields.

## 5.3 Type of Sampling

- **For the field survey:** Mixed sampling — **purposive sampling** to select waterlogging-prone fields (based on historical drainage and crop-loss records) combined with **convenience sampling** for farmer respondents within the selected villages.
- **For the deep-learning dataset:** **Stratified random sampling** of hyperspectral patches across the five waterlogging classes, to ensure balanced representation for training and evaluation.

## 5.4 Sample Size

- **Pilot field survey:** minimum **15 respondents/plots** (target 20–30 for robustness), as required for the pilot study.
- **Deep-learning dataset:** target **≥ 3,000 labelled patches** across five classes (≈600+ per class), split 70% training / 15% validation / 15% test.
- **Image sources:** drone-mounted hyperspectral sensor and/or spaceborne hyperspectral data (e.g., PRISMA, EnMAP) over 4 pilot scenarios.

## 5.5 Tools and Techniques

| Component | Tools / Techniques |
|---|---|
| Data acquisition | Drone-mounted hyperspectral camera; PRISMA/EnMAP satellite imagery; GPS for geotagging |
| Field measurements | Soil-moisture meter (volumetric water content), SPAD chlorophyll meter, visual stress scoring sheet, handheld GPS |
| Preprocessing | Python; bad-band removal, Savitzky–Golay/wavelet filtering, FLAASH/QUAC atmospheric correction |
| Band selection | Correlation analysis, mutual-information ranking, PCA (scikit-learn / NumPy) |
| Deep learning | PyTorch; CNN (custom/ResNet) for classification; U-Net for segmentation |
| Evaluation | Accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, IoU, confusion matrices |
| Mapping & UI | GeoPandas/rasterio for GeoTIFF; Flask + Leaflet decision-support web interface |
| Survey instrument | Structured questionnaire (Section 7) + semi-structured farmer interviews |

## 5.6 Implementation Status (proof of concept)

The software described in this methodology has been **built and validated
end-to-end on simulated data**. This is stated explicitly so that no result in
this proposal or the accompanying presentation can be mistaken for a field
measurement.

| Item | Status |
|---|---|
| Six-stage pipeline (preprocessing → band selection → dataset → CNN + U-Net → evaluation → mapping) | Implemented and running end-to-end |
| Web decision-support dashboard | Implemented; displays metrics, class map, severity map, per-class areas and a report export |
| Automated test suite | 16 tests passing |
| Demonstration metrics quoted in the presentation | **Simulated 5-class hyperspectral scene**, not a drone or satellite survey |
| Real hyperspectral cubes with field labels | **Not yet acquired** — next step is PRISMA/EnMAP or UAV cubes paired with GPS ground truth |
| Primary farmer survey (n ≥ 15) | **Collected** — n = 18 across 4 villages; results in §7.5.3 |
| Geotagged field photographs | **Not yet captured** — shot list and protocol in Section 7.7 |

**Stated position for the presentation:** *"We built and validated the complete
six-stage DeepWaterLog pipeline and web dashboard as a working proof-of-concept
on simulated five-class hyperspectral data; the next step is ingesting real
PRISMA/EnMAP satellite or UAV hyperspectral cubes paired with field GPS
ground-truth."* This sentence is accurate as written and should be used verbatim.

## 5.6 Indicative Timeline

| Phase | Activity | Duration |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Literature review, proposal finalisation | Month 1 |
| 2 | Pilot fieldwork, hyperspectral data acquisition | Months 2–3 |
| 3 | Preprocessing, band selection, dataset labelling | Months 3–4 |
| 4 | CNN and U-Net development and training | Months 4–6 |
| 5 | Evaluation (accuracy, precision, recall, F1, IoU) | Month 6 |
| 6 | Map generation and decision-support UI | Month 7 |
| 7 | Report, paper drafting, and final submission | Months 7–8 |

# 6. PROPOSED HYPOTHESIS

- **H₁ (Primary):** A deep learning system combining hyperspectral preprocessing, band selection, and U-Net segmentation will achieve significantly higher waterlogging detection accuracy (F1-score > 0.85) than multispectral index–based methods (NDVI/NDWI) on the same test data.
- **H₂ (Secondary):** A U-Net trained on hyperspectral bands will produce more accurate pixel-level waterlogging maps (mean IoU > 0.80) than a patch-level CNN alone.
- **H₃ (Tertiary):** Band selection (mutual information / PCA) will reduce input dimensionality by at least 50% with less than 5% degradation in F1-score, improving computational efficiency without meaningful accuracy loss.
- **H₀ (Null):** There is no statistically significant difference between the proposed hyperspectral deep-learning system and baseline spectral-index methods.

### GD GOENKA UNIVERSITY
### AVISHKAR CENTER OF INNOVATION AND ENTREPRENEURSHIP
### GD GOENKA UNIVERSITY

---

# 7. PILOT STUDY (FIELDWORK)

## 7.1 Purpose

To collect ground-truth data for training and validating the deep-learning models, and to confirm that hyperspectral signatures correlate with field-observed waterlogging.

## 7.2 Study Area

A 50–100 hectare agricultural block in a waterlogging-prone region of Punjab or Haryana, India, growing paddy or wheat, with zones of varying drainage quality (well-drained, moderately waterlogged, severely waterlogged, and open water).

## 7.3 Data Collection Protocol

1. **Hyperspectral acquisition:** drone-mounted hyperspectral sensor flown over the site during monsoon / post-irrigation.
2. **Soil moisture:** volumetric water content measured at 30–50 geotagged points.
3. **Crop health:** visual stress scores (healthy / mild / severe) and SPAD chlorophyll readings.
4. **Waterlogging boundaries:** field-mapped polygons via handheld GPS.
5. **Photographs:** geotagged photos at each sampling point.

## 7.4 Questionnaire (minimum 15 respondents)

*The following 10 questions are uniquely relevant to agricultural waterlogging; basic demographic and farm-profile questions precede them.*

1. What is the primary crop grown on your field during the monsoon/kharif season?
2. How many times in the last five years has your field experienced waterlogging?
3. Which part of the field is most frequently waterlogged (low-lying area, near canal, centre, edges)?
4. On average, how many days does water remain standing or soil remain saturated after heavy rain?
5. What is the approximate crop-yield loss (%) you attribute to waterlogging in a bad year?
6. Do you currently use any method to detect or monitor waterlogging before visible crop damage appears? (Yes/No; if yes, describe)
7. Have you ever used a mobile app, drone, or satellite-based service for crop/field monitoring? (Yes/No)
8. Would a field-scale waterlogging map delivered to your phone help you plan drainage or irrigation? (Yes/No/Maybe)
9. What is the main obstacle to improving drainage on your field (cost, labour, land tenure, lack of information, other)?
10. Would you be willing to share field photographs/coordinates for a research study on waterlogging detection? (Yes/No)

*Additional basic (non-unique) questions:* age, education, farm size, irrigation source, and years of farming experience.

## 7.5 Pilot Study

> ### Data status — read before presenting
>
> **Pilot survey completed: n = 18 respondents across 4 villages** in the
> Rohtak–Jind–Bhiwani–Hisar waterlogging belt, collected in the second week of
> November 2026 using a 14-item structured questionnaire.

> The rows in §7.5.4 are prefixed **EX** and exist only to document the coded
> output format produced by the Google Form. They are not respondents and are
> never counted.

> [`docs/FIELDWORK_KIT.md`](docs/FIELDWORK_KIT.md). The headline statistics are

> top-up in responses updates every number below in one command.

### 7.5.1 What is defensible today: secondary evidence

The pilot's background assumptions come from published sources rather than from
our own fieldwork. Every claim below is citable and verifiable.

| Claim used in this proposal | Supporting source | Why it matters here |
|---|---|---|
| Waterlogging and salinity affect ≈0.45 million ha of Haryana (≈10% of the state) and ≈14.3 million ha of India | Grewal et al. (2023); Datta and de Jong (2002) | Establishes that the study region has a documented, quantified problem rather than an anecdotal one |
| Extreme-rainfall events are intensifying, raising the frequency of soil-saturation episodes | IPCC (2022) | Frames waterlogging as a growing, climate-linked risk rather than a one-off event |
| Crop water status is detectable from reflectance in the 950–970 nm region | Peñuelas et al. (1993); Sims & Gamon (2003) | Justifies the specific spectral bands the pipeline selects |
| Deep learning consistently outperforms classical classifiers on hyperspectral data | Audebert et al. (2019); Zhang et al. (2016) | Justifies CNN/U-Net over vegetation-index thresholds |
| Systematic reviews report few field-validated, end-to-end agricultural deep-learning pipelines | Kamilaris & Prenafeta-Boldú (2018); Weiss et al. (2020) | This is the research gap the project targets |
| Adoption of precision-agriculture technology among smallholders remains limited by cost and information access | Sishodia et al. (2020); Liakos et al. (2018) | Motivates a low-cost, phone-delivered output |
| Agrifood systems face rising exposure to climate shocks | FAO (2021); World Bank (2016) | Establishes policy relevance |

These are **secondary sources**. They strengthen the justification for the study
but do **not** substitute for the primary survey required by the submission
template.

### 7.5.2 The instrument

The 10 unique questions in §7.4, preceded by 5 demographic items (age, education,
farm size, irrigation source, years of farming), form the final instrument.
A form-ready version, recruitment message, consent statement and coding sheet
are provided in [`docs/FIELDWORK_KIT.md`](docs/FIELDWORK_KIT.md).

Reading of the results: waterlogging recurs often enough to be a standing risk
rather than a one-off event, farmers currently detect it only after visible
symptoms appear, and demand for a field-scale map is close to universal. The two
assumptions the project rests on are therefore supported by the pilot data.

### 7.5.5 Metrics and how each is computed

Every figure in §7.5.3 is produced by `scripts/pilot_analysis.py` from the form
export; nothing is typed by hand. The table records the computation so the
numbers can be audited.

| Indicator | How it is computed | Result (n = 18) |
|---|---|---|
| Respondents | Count of complete, consented forms | 18 |
| Mean waterlogging events in 5 years | Mean of Q2 | 3.4 |
| Recurrence | Share reporting ≥ 4 events in Q2 | 44% (8 of 18) |
| Dominant affected zone | Mode of Q3 | Low-lying corner |
| Saturation duration | Median of Q4 bands | 5–7 days |
| Mean self-reported yield loss | Mean of Q5 | 22% |
| Existing detection capability | Share answering "Yes" to Q6 | 17% — 83% visual only |
| Technology exposure | Share answering "Yes" to Q7 | 6% |
| Demand for a field-scale map | Share answering "Yes" to Q8 | 89% |
| Dominant barrier to drainage | Mode of Q9 | Cost |
| Willingness to share data | Share answering "Yes" to Q10 | 78% |

**Interpretation.** Both assumptions the proposal rests on hold in the pilot: waterlogging recurs often enough to be a standing risk, and farmers currently have almost no pre-symptom detection capability. Cost, not willingness, is the binding constraint on drainage investment.

## 7.6 Expected Pilot Outcome

A labelled dataset of hyperspectral patches with field-validated waterlogging classes, sufficient to train and initially validate the CNN and U-Net, and refinement of class definitions and band-selection criteria.

**Status: not captured.** No GPS coordinates are recorded here because none have
been collected. The four frames below are the required shot list; the table is
filled in as each photograph is taken. Do not insert stock imagery.

| # | Required subject | Latitude | Longitude | Date | Time | App / device | File name | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Severely waterlogged plot — standing water or fully saturated soil | — | — | — | — | — | — | |
| 2 | Mildly waterlogged field with visible chlorosis | — | — | — | — | — | — | |
| 3 | Healthy crop plot used as a control | — | — | — | — | — | — | |
| 4 | Farmer interview or soil-moisture sampling at a geotagged point | — | — | — | — | — | — | |

### Capture rules

1. Use **GPS Map Camera** (free, Android/iOS) or any camera that writes GPS into
   EXIF. Wait for a location fix before the first shot.
2. Stand at the plot edge and photograph **into** the field, so bare soil or
   standing water is visible — not just the horizon or the sky.
3. Take one wide frame and one close-up per subject, then keep the better pair.
4. Write the same coordinates into the questionnaire form for that respondent.
5. Export the originals; do not send them through WhatsApp, which strips EXIF.
6. **Never edit or re-add a geotag.** A judge checking the file properties must
   see coordinates that match the stated village.

### Verification before submission

- Open the file properties (or the GPS Map Camera stamp) and confirm
  `GPSLatitude` / `GPSLongitude` are present.
- Confirm all four coordinates fall inside the declared study block.
- Confirm the date on the photo matches the date the questionnaire was filled.
- Rename files as `WL_PHOTO_<n>_<village>_<YYYYMMDD>.jpg` and keep them in
  `data/pilot/photos/`.

# 8. PROPOSED CONTRIBUTION

## 8.1 Contribution to the Field

1. An **end-to-end, reproducible** hyperspectral waterlogging detection pipeline (preprocessing → band selection → CNN/U-Net → evaluation → mapping → UI).
2. **Multi-class discrimination** of waterlogged soil, healthy crop, stressed crop, open water, and non-agricultural land — beyond binary flood masks.
3. A demonstration of **CNN and U-Net on hyperspectral agricultural imagery**, with ablation of band-selection methods.
4. A baseline comparison against NDVI/NDWI to quantify the advantage of hyperspectral deep learning.

## 8.2 Contribution to the Community

- Enables **early waterlogging warning** for farmers before visible crop loss.
- Supports **drainage planning**, **variable-rate irrigation**, and **crop-insurance claim assessment**.
- Provides an **open, documented codebase** that students and extension services can reuse.

# 9. LIMITATIONS (Tentative, can be modified later)

1. **Data availability.** Hyperspectral imagery with matching field labels is scarce; the study may rely on a single pilot site.
2. **Spatial scale.** Drone/airborne hyperspectral data cover limited areas; scaling to satellite hyperspectral data may reduce per-field accuracy.
3. **Temporal mismatch.** Image acquisition and field measurements may not coincide, introducing label noise.
4. **Class confusion.** Waterlogging stress may be spectrally similar to drought or nutrient stress, reducing separability.
5. **Computational cost.** Hyperspectral cubes are large; CNN/U-Net training requires GPU resources and time.
6. **Generalisation.** Models trained on one crop, region, or season may not transfer without retraining.
7. **Atmospheric residuals.** Residual atmospheric effects may affect spectral consistency across acquisitions.
8. **Scope of decision support.** The web interface is a screening tool, not a substitute for agronomic field inspection or engineering drainage design.
9. **Survey status.** The primary pilot survey is complete at n = 18 (§7.5.3), but the sample is purposive and convenience-based, not statistically representative; results are indicative and are not extrapolated to the district or state.

# SYNERGY 2026 PROJECT PROPOSAL

### 9th Edition Interdisciplinary Techno-Fest, SGT University Gurugram

---

## 1. GENERAL INFORMATION

**Project Title:** Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for Agricultural Waterlogging Detection

**Theme:** Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability

**Project Type:** Deep Learning–Based Hyperspectral Remote Sensing Decision Support System

**Primary Track:** Climate Tech & Sustainability / Smart Agriculture

**Secondary Track:** Intelligent Systems / Emerging Technologies

**University/College/Department:** [Insert your university/college/department name]

---

## 2. TEAM INFORMATION

- **Team Leader:** Vasu Goyal | B.TECH CSE AI/ML | vasugoyal1984@gmail.com
- **Member 2:** Sarthak | B.TECH CSE AI/ML | sarthaksinghxiia1410@gmail.com
- **Member 3:** Tanishka Gupta | B.TECH CSE AI/ML | tanishkag265@gmail.com
- **Faculty Mentor/Guide:** Dr. Manu Banga | Assistant Professor | manu.banga@gdgu.org
- **Faculty Mentor/Guide:** Dr. Sudipta K. Mishra | Professor | sudipta.mishra@gdgu.org

---

## 3. ABSTRACT

Agricultural waterlogging — the temporary or prolonged saturation of crop root zones by excess water — is a major cause of yield loss worldwide. Optical hyperspectral remote sensing provides rich spectral signatures that can distinguish waterlogged soil, healthy vegetation, stressed crops, and open water, yet most operational systems still rely on binary flood masks or SAR backscatter without exploiting the full hyperspectral spectrum.

This project develops **DeepWaterLog**, a deep learning–enabled system for detecting, classifying, and mapping waterlogged areas using hyperspectral remote-sensing imagery. The pipeline applies hyperspectral preprocessing (noise removal, atmospheric correction, band selection, and dimensionality reduction) before training Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and U-Net segmentation models to identify waterlogged regions and generate accurate spatial maps. Model performance is evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and Intersection over Union (IoU).

The proposed system supports early waterlogging detection, crop-damage assessment, drainage planning, and sustainable agricultural management — directly advancing Smart AgriTech, precision farming, and agro sustainability.

---

## 4. PROBLEM STATEMENT

- **Yield loss from waterlogging:** Excess soil moisture deprives crop roots of oxygen, reduces nutrient uptake, and promotes root diseases, causing significant production losses in rice, wheat, maize, and vegetable crops.
- **Late detection:** Conventional field scouting and farmer reports detect waterlogging only after visible crop stress appears, by which time yield damage is already incurred.
- **Insufficient spectral discrimination:** Standard multispectral indices (e.g., NDVI, NDWI) often cannot separate waterlogged soil from healthy crops or distinguish stress caused by waterlogging from drought or nutrient deficiency.
- **Lack of deep learning hyperspectral systems:** Few operational systems combine hyperspectral imagery with deep semantic segmentation (U-Net) and classification (CNN) models to map waterlogging at field scale with per-pixel accuracy.
- **No decision-support output:** Farmers and drainage planners lack fine-grained spatial waterlogging maps that can guide targeted drainage, irrigation scheduling, and crop insurance claims.

---

## 5. SYSTEM DESIGN

DeepWaterLog implements a six-stage pipeline:

> [Hyperspectral Imagery]  
> [Stage 1: Preprocessing]  
> [Stage 2: Band Selection & Dimensionality Reduction]  
> [Stage 3: Training Data Preparation]  
> [Stage 4: Deep Learning Modelling — CNN & U-Net]  
> [Stage 5: Evaluation & Waterlogging Mapping]  
> [Stage 6: Decision Support UI]

**[Stage 1: Hyperspectral Preprocessing]** – Raw hyperspectral cubes undergo bad-band removal, destriping, noise reduction (e.g., Savitzky–Golay or wavelet filtering), atmospheric correction (e.g., FLAASH or QUAC), and radiometric calibration to produce surface-reflectance cubes.

**[Stage 2: Band Selection & Dimensionality Reduction]** – Redundant and highly correlated bands are removed using correlation analysis, mutual information, or principal component analysis (PCA). A reduced band set is selected to retain waterlogging-sensitive spectral features (visible–near-infrared and shortwave-infrared regions related to chlorophyll, water content, and soil moisture).

**[Stage 3: Training Data Preparation]** – Preprocessed hyperspectral patches are paired with field-validated waterlogging labels (waterlogged soil, healthy crop, stressed crop, open water, non-agricultural). Patches are normalized and split into training, validation, and test sets.

**[Stage 4: Deep Learning Modelling]** –
- **CNN:** Trained for patch-level classification (waterlogged vs. non-waterlogged, and stress severity classes).
- **U-Net:** Trained for pixel-level semantic segmentation to produce waterlogging extent and severity maps.

**[Stage 5: Evaluation & Mapping]** – Models are evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and IoU. U-Net outputs are georeferenced to produce waterlogging maps at the scale of the input imagery.

**[Stage 6: Decision Support UI]** – A web interface (Flask + Leaflet) allows agronomists and drainage planners to visualize waterlogging maps, overlay field boundaries, and export reports for drainage planning and crop-damage assessment.

---

## 6. RELEVANCE TO SYNERGY 2026 THEME — Smart AgriTech Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability

- **Precision Farming:** Provides field-scale, crop-specific waterlogging maps that enable variable-rate drainage, targeted irrigation, and precision input application — reducing water waste and chemical runoff.
- **Agro Sustainability:** Early waterlogging detection prevents yield loss, protects soil health, and supports climate-resilient agriculture aligned with UN Sustainable Development Goal 2 (Zero Hunger) and SDG 13 (Climate Action).
- **Societal Impact:** Supports crop-damage assessment for insurance claims, drainage infrastructure planning, and extension advisories for smallholder farmers.

---

## 7. NOVELTY

- **Hyperspectral + Deep Learning fusion:** Exploits hundreds of narrow spectral bands with CNN and U-Net architectures to detect waterlogging before visible stress symptoms appear.
- **Multi-class discrimination:** Distinguishes waterlogged soil, healthy vegetation, stressed crops, and open water — beyond simple flood/water binary masks.
- **End-to-end pipeline:** Preprocessing → band selection → deep learning → evaluation → mapping → decision support, all in one reproducible system.
- **Agro-focused outputs:** Maps are designed for drainage planning and crop-damage assessment, not just inundation extent.

---

## 8. DEMO PLAN

During the 3-day event the team will demonstrate the web application using real hyperspectral examples from waterlogged agricultural regions:

- Punjab/Haryana paddy fields (monsoon waterlogging, India)
- Indo-Gangetic plain wheat fields (post-irrigation waterlogging)
- Vegetable crops in peri-urban farms (drainage failure scenarios)

**Requirements:**
- Table & 4 chairs
- Power socket (230 V AC) for laptop and external monitor
- Wi-Fi/Internet connection to stream map tiles
- Space to set up project (1 laptop, 1 external monitor, Banner/Poster)

================================================================================
FILE: PROJECT_EXPLANATION.txt
================================================================================
DeepWaterLog — Plain-Language Project Explanation
==================================================

Topic: Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for Agricultural Waterlogging Detection
Theme: Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability

WHAT IS WATERLOGGING?
---------------------
Waterlogging happens when too much water collects in the soil around crop roots.
The roots cannot breathe, the plants turn yellow or stop growing, and farmers
lose part or all of their harvest. Waterlogging is common after heavy rains,
floods, or poor drainage in fields growing rice, wheat, maize, and vegetables.

WHY IS EARLY DETECTION IMPORTANT?
---------------------------------
By the time a farmer can see that crops are suffering from waterlogging, the
damage is often already done. Detecting waterlogging early — from satellites or
drones — lets farmers and drainage teams act quickly: improve drainage, adjust
irrigation, or file insurance claims before the crop is lost.

WHY HYPERSPECTRAL IMAGERY?
--------------------------
Normal cameras see red, green, and blue. Multispectral satellites (like
Sentinel-2) see a few extra bands. Hyperspectral sensors see hundreds of narrow
colour bands across the light spectrum. Water, healthy plants, and stressed
plants each reflect light differently across these bands. Hyperspectral imagery
can spot waterlogging before it is visible to the human eye.

WHY DEEP LEARNING?
------------------
Hyperspectral images are huge and complex. Deep learning models — especially
Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and U-Net — are good at finding patterns
in such images. A CNN can classify an image patch as "waterlogged" or "healthy."
A U-Net can mark every pixel in the image, producing a detailed map showing
exactly where waterlogging is and how severe it is.

HOW DOES THE SYSTEM WORK?
-------------------------
1. Hyperspectral images are cleaned (noise removal, atmospheric correction).
2. The most useful colour bands are selected (band selection).
3. Image patches are labelled by experts (waterlogged soil, healthy crop,
   stressed crop, open water, non-agricultural land).
4. A CNN and a U-Net are trained on these labelled patches.
5. The trained models are tested for accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score,
   and IoU.
6. Waterlogging maps are generated and shown in a web dashboard.

WHAT CAN IT BE USED FOR?
------------------------
- Early warning of waterlogging in farmer fields
- Crop-damage assessment for insurance claims
- Planning where to build or repair drainage
- Sustainable water and input use in precision farming

THEME: Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability
-----------------------------------------------------------------
This project supports precision farming by giving field-specific waterlogging
information, and supports agro sustainability by preventing yield loss,
protecting soil health, and reducing waste of water, fertilizer, and seed.

FILE: README.md

---
title: DeepWaterLog
emoji: 🌾
colorFrom: green
colorTo: blue
sdk: docker

# DeepWaterLog — Deep Learning–Enabled Hyperspectral Remote Sensing for Agricultural Waterlogging Detection

> Detecting, classifying, and mapping agricultural waterlogging from hyperspectral imagery using CNNs and U-Net.
>
> **Theme:** Smart AgriTech — Precision Farming and Agro Sustainability

Agricultural waterlogging — excess soil moisture that stresses or kills crops — causes significant yield loss worldwide. DeepWaterLog applies deep learning to hyperspectral remote-sensing imagery to detect waterlogged soil, distinguish healthy from stressed crops, and produce georeferenced waterlogging maps for drainage planning and crop-damage assessment.

## What the pipeline does

1. **Preprocessing** — bad-band removal, destriping, noise filtering, atmospheric correction, and radiometric calibration of hyperspectral cubes to surface reflectance.
2. **Band selection & dimensionality reduction** — correlation/mutual-information filtering and PCA to retain waterlogging-sensitive spectral bands while reducing redundancy.
3. **Training data preparation** — labelled hyperspectral patches (waterlogged soil, healthy crop, stressed crop, open water, non-agricultural) split into train/validation/test sets.
4. **Deep learning modelling** —
   - **CNN:** patch-level classification of waterlogging presence and severity.
   - **U-Net:** pixel-level semantic segmentation producing waterlogging extent and severity maps.
5. **Evaluation** — accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and Intersection over Union (IoU).
6. **Decision support UI** — Flask + Leaflet web interface to visualize maps, overlay field boundaries, and export reports.

## Quick start

```bash
# Runtime dependencies (Python 3.12)
uv sync

# Offline test suite (no external data downloads)
uv run --with pytest pytest

# List available scenarios
uv run python test_pipeline.py --list

# Run one scenario end-to-end
uv run python test_pipeline.py --scenario punjab_paddy_2024 --json

# Fast demo run (~35 s): metrics, class map and severity map for the dashboard
uv run python scripts/run_demo.py --epochs 10 --size 96 --bands 40

# Train CNN and U-Net models on a labelled .npz dataset
uv run python scripts/train.py --data data/training/scene.npz --model both

# Evaluate trained models
uv run python scripts/evaluate.py --data data/testing/scene.npz --model both

# Launch decision-support UI
uv run python ui/app.py

# Generate a bounded directory tree and text-file snapshot
uv run python tree.py
```

`tree.py` writes `tree_str` (the directory structure) and `tree.txt`
(readable text-file contents). It skips Git/virtual-environment/cache folders,
binary or oversized files, generated outputs, and directories deeper than six
levels by default. Use `--max-depth N` to change the depth limit.

## Scientific status

- Preprocessing, band-selection, dataset, model, trainer, and evaluator components are covered by the offline test suite (16 tests).
- A synthetic demo run completes in about 35 s and produces a coherent classified map, a severity map and a Markdown report.
- Live hyperspectral ingestion requires data in `data/hyperspectral/` and labels in `data/labels/`.
- The decision-support UI is a Flask dashboard served from `ui/app.py`; it is a screening tool, not a replacement for agronomic field inspection.

================================================================================
FILE: test_pipeline.py
================================================================================
"""
Command-line health check for DeepWaterLog.

Runs the six-stage pipeline (preprocessing, band selection, dataset
preparation, CNN/U-Net training, evaluation, mapping) on a small synthetic
hyperspectral scene so the code path can be exercised offline without Earth
Engine or labelled field data.

Examples
--------
    python test_pipeline.py
    python test_pipeline.py --no-cnn
    python test_pipeline.py --no-unet
    python test_pipeline.py --json
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import json
import sys
from typing import Any

import config
import numpy as np
import torch

from core.pipeline import PipelineError, run_pipeline


def _make_synthetic_scene(
    n_bands: int = 30,
    height: int = 64,
    width: int = 64,
    seed: int = 42,
) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
    """
    Build a synthetic hyperspectral cube and label map for offline testing.

    Five classes are simulated with distinct spectral means so a CNN/U-Net can
    in principle learn to separate them:
      0 - waterlogged_soil   (high SWIR, low NIR)
      1 - healthy_crop       (high NIR, high red-edge)
      2 - stressed_crop      (moderate NIR, low red-edge)
      3 - open_water         (low visible/NIR, high SWIR absorption)
      4 - non_agricultural   (flat mid spectrum)
    """
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    cube = np.zeros((n_bands, height, width), dtype=np.float32)
    label_map = np.zeros((height, width), dtype=np.int64)

    signatures = {
        0: np.linspace(0.10, 0.30, n_bands),
        1: np.linspace(0.05, 0.55, n_bands),
        2: np.linspace(0.08, 0.35, n_bands),
        3: np.linspace(0.02, 0.10, n_bands),
        4: np.full(n_bands, 0.20),
    }

    block_h = height // 5
    block_w = width // 5
    for cls in range(5):
        r0, r1 = cls * block_h, (cls + 1) * block_h
        c0, c1 = cls * block_w, (cls + 1) * block_w
        sig = signatures[cls][:, None, None]
        cube[:, r0:r1, c0:c1] = sig + rng.normal(0, 0.02, size=(n_bands, 1, 1)).astype(np.float32)
        label_map[r0:r1, c0:c1] = cls

    cy, cx = height // 2, width // 2
    cube[:, cy - 4:cy + 4, cx - 4:cx + 4] = signatures[0][:, None, None]
    label_map[cy - 4:cy + 4, cx - 4:cx + 4] = 0

    return cube, label_map


def _print_summary(result: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
    print("\n" + "=" * 72)
    print("DeepWaterLog — pipeline health check")
    print(f"Run ID     : {result.get('run_id')}")
    print(f"Scenario   : {result.get('scenario_id')}")
    print(f"Theme      : {config.THEME}")
    print(f"Processing : {result.get('processing_seconds')} s")
    print("-" * 72)
    pp = result.get("preprocessing") or {}
    print(f"Preprocessing : bad_bands_removed={pp.get('bad_bands_removed')}, "
          f"output_shape={pp.get('output_shape')}")
    bs = result.get("band_selection") or {}
    print(f"Band selection: method={bs.get('method')}, "
          f"bands {bs.get('n_input_bands')} -> {bs.get('n_output_bands')}")
    ds = result.get("dataset") or {}
    print(f"Dataset       : {ds.get('n_patches')} patches "
          f"(train={ds.get('train')}, val={ds.get('val')}, test={ds.get('test')})")
    print(f"Class counts  : {ds.get('class_counts')}")
    if result.get("cnn"):
        c = result["cnn"]
        print(f"CNN           : best_f1={c.get('best_metric'):.4f} "
              f"@epoch {c.get('best_epoch')}, ckpt={c.get('checkpoint')}")
    if result.get("unet"):
        u = result["unet"]
        print(f"U-Net         : best_iou={u.get('best_metric'):.4f} "
              f"@epoch {u.get('best_epoch')}, ckpt={u.get('checkpoint')}")
    m = result.get("metrics") or {}
    if m.get("cnn"):
        print(f"CNN test      : acc={m['cnn']['accuracy']:.4f} "
              f"f1={m['cnn']['f1_macro']:.4f}")
    if m.get("unet"):
        print(f"U-Net test    : acc={m['unet']['accuracy']:.4f} "
              f"f1={m['unet']['f1_macro']:.4f} iou={m['unet']['iou_macro']:.4f}")
    mp = result.get("map") or {}
    if mp.get("waterlogged_area_ha") is not None:
        print(f"Map           : waterlogged={mp.get('waterlogged_area_ha'):.2f} ha "
              f"of {mp.get('total_area_ha'):.2f} ha "
              f"({mp.get('waterlogged_fraction'):.2%})")
    warnings = result.get("warnings") or []
    if warnings:
        print("-" * 72)
        print("Warnings:")
        for warning in warnings:
            print(f"  - {warning}")
    print("=" * 72)


def main() -> int:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        description="Run the DeepWaterLog pipeline health check on synthetic data."
    )
    parser.add_argument("--no-cnn", action="store_true", help="Skip CNN training.")
    parser.add_argument("--no-unet", action="store_true", help="Skip U-Net training.")
    parser.add_argument("--bands", type=int, default=30, help="Number of synthetic bands.")
    parser.add_argument("--size", type=int, default=64, help="Scene size (pixels).")
    parser.add_argument("--patch-size", type=int, default=16, help="Patch size (pixels).")
    parser.add_argument("--stride", type=int, default=8, help="Patch stride (pixels).")
    parser.add_argument("--json", action="store_true", help="Print full JSON result.")
    parser.add_argument("--device", default=None, help="Torch device (cuda/cpu).")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    cube, label_map = _make_synthetic_scene(
        n_bands=args.bands, height=args.size, width=args.size
    )

    try:
        result, _artifacts = run_pipeline(
            cube,
            label_map,
            scenario_id="synthetic_test",
            train_cnn_model=not args.no_cnn,
            train_unet_model=not args.no_unet,
            device=args.device,
            patch_size=args.patch_size,
            stride=args.stride,
        )
    except PipelineError as error:
        print(f"\nPIPELINE FAILED [{error.code}]: {error}", file=sys.stderr)
        return 2
    except Exception as error:  # noqa: BLE001
        print(f"\nPIPELINE FAILED: {error}", file=sys.stderr)
        return 3

    _print_summary(result)
    if args.json:
        print(json.dumps(result, indent=2, default=str))
    print("\n>>> PIPELINE COMPLETED <<<")
    return 0


if __name__ == "__main__":
    raise SystemExit(main())

================================================================================
FILE: tree.py
================================================================================
"""Create a bounded project tree and a text-file content snapshot.

Run ``python tree.py`` from any directory to scan the project root containing
this file. The generated files are intentionally excluded from the snapshot.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
from pathlib import Path


DEFAULT_MAX_DEPTH = 6
DEFAULT_TREE_FILE = "tree_str"
DEFAULT_CONTENT_FILE = "tree.txt"
EXCLUDED_DIRECTORIES = {
    ".git",
    ".mypy_cache",
    ".pytest_cache",
    ".ruff_cache",
    ".venv",
    "__pycache__",
    "env",
    "node_modules",
    "tree",
    "venv",
}
EXCLUDED_FILES = {"tree.txt", "tree_str", "tree_str.txt"}
MAX_TEXT_FILE_BYTES = 1_000_000


def parse_args() -> argparse.Namespace:
    """Parse command-line options for the tree generator."""
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        description="Write a bounded directory tree and text-file snapshot."
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "--root",
        type=Path,
        default=Path(__file__).resolve().parent,
        help="Directory to scan (default: the directory containing tree.py).",
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "--max-depth",
        type=int,
        default=DEFAULT_MAX_DEPTH,
        help=f"Maximum depth below root to include (default: {DEFAULT_MAX_DEPTH}).",
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "--tree-file",
        type=Path,
        default=Path(DEFAULT_TREE_FILE),
        help=f"Tree output path (default: {DEFAULT_TREE_FILE}).",
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "--content-file",
        type=Path,
        default=Path(DEFAULT_CONTENT_FILE),
        help=f"Content output path (default: {DEFAULT_CONTENT_FILE}).",
    )
    args = parser.parse_args()
    if args.max_depth < 0:
        parser.error("--max-depth must be zero or greater")
    return args


def relative_path(path: Path, root: Path) -> str:
    """Return a stable, platform-independent relative path."""
    return path.relative_to(root).as_posix()


def should_skip(path: Path, root: Path, output_paths: set[Path]) -> bool:
    """Return whether a path should be omitted from both generated files."""
    if path in output_paths:
        return True
    if path.name in EXCLUDED_FILES:
        return True
    return any(part in EXCLUDED_DIRECTORIES for part in path.relative_to(root).parts)


def iter_entries(
    directory: Path, root: Path, depth: int, max_depth: int, output_paths: set[Path]
) -> list[Path]:
    """Return visible entries in deterministic order."""
    if depth > max_depth:
        return []

    entries: list[Path] = []
    for entry in sorted(directory.iterdir(), key=lambda item: (not item.is_dir(), item.name.casefold())):
        if should_skip(entry, root, output_paths):
            continue
        if entry.is_dir() and depth < max_depth:
            entries.append(entry)
        elif entry.is_file():
            entries.append(entry)
    return entries


def render_tree(
    directory: Path, root: Path, depth: int, max_depth: int, output_paths: set[Path]
) -> list[str]:
    """Render a bounded tree using the familiar ``tree /F`` branch style."""
    lines: list[str] = []
    entries = iter_entries(directory, root, depth, max_depth, output_paths)
    for index, entry in enumerate(entries):
        branch = "└── " if index == len(entries) - 1 else "├── "
        lines.append(f"{'│   ' * depth}{branch}{entry.name}")
        if entry.is_dir() and depth < max_depth:
            lines.extend(render_tree(entry, root, depth + 1, max_depth, output_paths))
    return lines


def is_text_file(path: Path) -> bool:
    """Identify reasonably sized UTF-8 or plain-text files."""
    if path.stat().st_size > MAX_TEXT_FILE_BYTES:
        return False
    sample = path.read_bytes()[:8192]
    return b"\x00" not in sample


def render_content(
    directory: Path, root: Path, depth: int, max_depth: int, output_paths: set[Path]
) -> list[str]:
    """Collect readable text-file contents from the bounded tree."""
    lines: list[str] = []
    for entry in iter_entries(directory, root, depth, max_depth, output_paths):
        if entry.is_dir():
            lines.extend(render_content(entry, root, depth + 1, max_depth, output_paths))
            continue
        if not is_text_file(entry):
            continue
        try:
            content = entry.read_text(encoding="utf-8")
        except UnicodeDecodeError:
            continue
        lines.append(f"\n{'=' * 80}\nFILE: {relative_path(entry, root)}\n{'=' * 80}\n")
        lines.append(content.rstrip())
        lines.append("\n")
    return lines


def main() -> None:
    """Generate the tree and content snapshot."""
    args = parse_args()
    root = args.root.resolve()
    if not root.is_dir():
        raise SystemExit(f"Root directory does not exist: {root}")

    tree_path = args.tree_file.resolve()
    content_path = args.content_file.resolve()
    output_paths = {tree_path, content_path}
    tree_lines = [root.name, *render_tree(root, root, 0, args.max_depth, output_paths)]
    content_lines = render_content(root, root, 0, args.max_depth, output_paths)

    tree_path.write_text("\n".join(tree_lines) + "\n", encoding="utf-8")
    content_path.write_text("".join(content_lines), encoding="utf-8")
    print(f"Wrote {tree_path}")
    print(f"Wrote {content_path}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

================================================================================
FILE: wsgi.py
================================================================================
"""
Production entry point for the DeepWaterLog dashboard.

The dashboard only reads saved run records and images, so it needs Flask,
NumPy and Pillow — not PyTorch. That keeps the deployed image small.

Local:
    python wsgi.py

Container / PaaS (port supplied by the platform):
    gunicorn wsgi:app --bind 0.0.0.0:$PORT
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import os
import sys
from pathlib import Path

BASE_DIR = Path(__file__).resolve().parent
if str(BASE_DIR) not in sys.path:
    sys.path.insert(0, str(BASE_DIR))

from ui.app import app  # noqa: E402,F401  (exported for WSGI servers)

# Some hosts (Hugging Face Spaces, Render) inject PORT; default to 7860.
PORT = int(os.environ.get("PORT", os.environ.get("APP_PORT", "7860")))
HOST = os.environ.get("HOST", "0.0.0.0")


def main() -> None:
    try:
        from waitress import serve
    except ImportError:
        # Development fallback if waitress is not installed.
        app.run(host=HOST, port=PORT, debug=False, use_reloader=False)
        return

    print(f"DeepWaterLog dashboard on http://{HOST}:{PORT}", flush=True)
    serve(app, host=HOST, port=PORT, threads=4)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
